
\medterm{16S Ribosomal RNA Sequencing} Molecular identification of bacteria and archaea using conserved and variable regions of
the 16S ribosomal RNA gene. It is useful for difficult-to-culture organisms but may not
distinguish closely related species or provide susceptibility data.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SAD} Seasonal affective disorder, a recurring mood disorder linked to seasonal changes and reduced daylight.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Seasonal affective disorder

\medterm{Saline} A sterile water solution containing sodium chloride, commonly used for fluids, irrigation, or drug delivery.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Normal saline

\medterm{Sarcolemma} The specialized cell membrane surrounding a muscle fiber.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Satisficer} A person who chooses an option that meets acceptable criteria without seeking the absolute best option.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sausage Digit} Diffuse swelling of an entire finger or toe, often seen with inflammatory arthritis.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Dactylitis

\medterm{Scapulothoracic Joint} The functional gliding connection between the scapula and the back of the chest wall.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schlemm’s Canal} A circular channel that drains aqueous humor from the eye.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Canal of Schlemm

\medterm{Scintillations} Perceived flashes or zigzag lines, often occurring during a migraine aura.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Osteoporosis} Bone loss caused by another illness, medication, or prolonged immobility.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sed Rate} A blood test measuring how quickly red blood cells settle, used as a nonspecific marker of inflammation.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Erythrocyte sedimentation rate; ESR

\medterm{Selective Estrogen Receptor Modulators} Medicines that activate estrogen receptors in some tissues and block them in others.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
SERMs

\medterm{Selective Serotonin Reuptake Inhibitors} Antidepressants that increase serotonin signaling by reducing its reuptake into nerve cells.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
SSRIs

\medterm{Self-Help Group} A peer group in which members support one another while changing a harmful behavior or coping with a problem.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Support group

\medterm{Seminal Vesicles} Glands behind the bladder that produce much of the fluid in semen.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Senescence} Gradual biological decline associated with aging.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Biological aging

\medterm{Senile Dementia} An outdated term formerly used for dementia in older adults.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Dementia

\medterm{Sensate Focus Techniques} Structured touch exercises used in sex therapy to reduce performance pressure and improve intimacy.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Sensate focus

\medterm{Sensitized} State of having preformed antibodies against human leukocyte antigens (HLA); complicates transplant matching and increases rejection risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SERMs} Selective estrogen receptor modulators that have estrogen-like effects in some tissues and blocking effects in others.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Selective estrogen receptor modulators

\medterm{Seroma} A pocket of clear fluid that develops near a surgical incision or injured tissue.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serotonin} A neurotransmitter involved in mood, sleep, appetite, and pain regulation.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
5-hydroxytryptamine; 5-HT

\medterm{Sesamoiditis} Inflammation and pain around a small sesamoid bone, often beneath the big toe.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Set} A group of consecutive repetitions of an exercise.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Short-Term Memory} The temporary storage and use of information over seconds to minutes.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Working memory

\medterm{Sigmoidoscopy} Sigmoidoscopy examines sigmoidoscopy; interpretation depends on the specimen or body region, test method, reference range, and clinical question.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Signature Strengths} Personal character strengths that a person values and naturally uses.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Silent Heart Attack} A myocardial infarction without recognized pain or typical warning symptoms.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Unrecognized myocardial infarction

\medterm{Silent Ischemia} Inadequate blood flow to heart muscle without noticeable symptoms.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Asymptomatic ischemia

\medterm{Single-Photon Absorptiometry} An older technique using gamma rays to measure bone density, usually in an arm or peripheral bone.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
SPA

\medterm{Sinus Node} The heart’s natural pacemaker, located in the right atrium.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Sinoatrial node

\medterm{Six Minute Walk} Functional exercise test measuring the distance a patient can walk in six minutes; used to assess cardiopulmonary capacity and functional status.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skeletal Muscles} Muscles attached to bones that produce voluntary movement.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Voluntary muscles

\medterm{Skin Resurfacing} Procedures that improve skin texture or pigmentation by removing or remodeling superficial skin layers.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Architecture} The pattern and organization of sleep stages across a sleep period.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Spindles} Brief bursts of rhythmic brain activity during stage 2 non-REM sleep.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sling} A supportive device or surgically placed strip used to support an injured limb or urinary structures.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Slipped Disk} A spinal disk that bulges or herniates through a tear in its outer layer.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Herniated disk

\medterm{Slipped Vertebra} Forward displacement of one vertebra relative to the one below it.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Spondylolisthesis

\medterm{Slit Lamp} An instrument using magnification and a narrow beam of light to examine the eye.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Biomicroscope

\medterm{Slow-Twitch Fiber} A muscle fiber adapted for sustained, aerobic activity and fatigue resistance.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Type I muscle fiber

\medterm{Slow-Wave Sleep} Deep non-REM sleep characterized by slow brain waves and reduced responsiveness.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Deep sleep

\medterm{SMART} A goal-setting framework emphasizing specific, measurable, achievable, realistic, and time-bound goals.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
SMART goals

\medterm{Social Worker} Healthcare professional providing psychosocial support, resource coordination, and counseling for patients and families throughout the transplant process.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatization} Expression of psychological distress through physical symptoms.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somniloquy} Talking during sleep.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Sleep talking

\medterm{Sorbitol} A sugar alcohol used as a sweetener and, in larger amounts, as an osmotic laxative.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spacer} A chamber used with a metered-dose inhaler to improve delivery of medicine to the lungs.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Valved holding chamber

\medterm{Sphincter} A ring-shaped muscle that regulates an opening by contracting and relaxing.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Tap} A procedure that withdraws cerebrospinal fluid from the lower spinal canal for testing.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Lumbar puncture

\medterm{Spinous Process} The posterior bony projection of a vertebra to which muscles and ligaments attach.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splenic Flexure Syndrome} Left-upper-abdominal pain caused by gas trapped at the bend between the transverse and descending colon.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spongy Bone} Porous trabecular bone found inside many bones, especially near joints.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Cancellous bone; trabecular bone

\medterm{Squamous Cell} A thin, flat epithelial cell.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stable Angina} Predictable exertional chest pain caused by temporary inadequate coronary blood flow.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Chronic stable angina

\medterm{Stable Coronary Artery Disease} Coronary artery narrowing causing a consistent, predictable pattern of angina.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Stable ischemic heart disease

\medterm{Stages of Change} A model describing gradual phases people pass through while changing behavior.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Transtheoretical model

\medterm{Standardized Extract} An herbal extract adjusted to contain a specified amount of a selected constituent.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Statins} Cholesterol-lowering medicines that inhibit HMG-CoA reductase.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors

\medterm{Steroids} Hormone-like medicines or compounds with a four-ring chemical structure; corticosteroids are used to reduce inflammation.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Corticosteroids

\medterm{Stimulant} A substance that increases activity of the central nervous system or other body systems.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stratum Corneum} The outermost layer of the epidermis, composed mainly of dead keratinized cells.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strength} The ability of a muscle or muscle group to exert force.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strength Training} Exercise using resistance to increase muscle strength and capacity.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Resistance training; weight training

\medterm{Stress Response} Physiological changes that prepare the body to respond to a perceived threat.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Fight-or-flight response

\medterm{Stressors} Events, conditions, or circumstances that trigger a stress response.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stria} A line, streak, or band in tissue, such as a stretch mark.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Striae

\medterm{Stricture} Abnormal narrowing of a hollow body passage.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subarachnoid Space} The space between the arachnoid and pia mater containing cerebrospinal fluid.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subcutaneous} Subcutaneous concerns subcutaneous; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
\textbf{Synonyms}:
Subcuticular

\medterm{Subendocardial Myocytes} Heart-muscle cells in the inner layer of the myocardium, which are especially vulnerable to reduced blood flow.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{S3 Gallop Pathology} A third heart sound produced during rapid ventricular filling. In some adults it may indicate
volume overload or ventricular dysfunction, although it can be physiologic in children,
young adults, and pregnancy. Its significance depends on the complete clinical
assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{S4 Gallop Pathology} A fourth heart sound indicates atrial contraction against a stiff ventricle (decreased
compliance). Associated with left ventricular hypertrophy from hypertension, aortic
stenosis, and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. S4 is best heard at the apex with the bell,
and is accentuated by exertion, expiration, and isorhythmic AV dissociation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sacral Nerve Stimulation} Neuromodulation for urinary/fecal incontinence, constipation. Sacral nerve root (S3)
stimulation. Implantable pulse generator + electrode. Indications: refractory urge
incontinence, fecal incontinence, non-obstructive urinary retention.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sacral Nerve Stimulator} An implanted device that delivers electrical stimulation to sacral nerves to alter bladder, bowel, or pelvic-floor function in
selected patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sacral Vertebrae} Five vertebrae fused to form the sacrum. They transmit body weight from the vertebral
column to the pelvic girdle and contain anterior and posterior sacral foramina for
spinal nerves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sacroiliac Joint} The synovial joint between the sacrum and the ilium (auricular surfaces), forming the
connection between the axial skeleton and the pelvis. The sacroiliac joint is reinforced
by strong ligaments (anterior, interosseous, and posterior sacroiliac ligaments,
sacrotuberous and sacrospinous ligaments).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sacroiliitis} Inflammation of one or both sacroiliac joints, causing pain in the lower back, buttock,
or posterior thigh that may worsen with prolonged rest. Causes include axial
spondyloarthritis, psoriatic or enteropathic arthritis, infection, trauma, and
degenerative disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sacrum} Large triangular bone from 5 fused sacral vertebrae between the hip bones. Articulates with L5 superiorly and coccyx inferiorly. The sacral promontory is an obstetric landmark. Contains 4 pairs of sacral foramina. Forms the posterior pelvic wall.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Safety Plan} A collaboratively written, practical plan for responding to escalating suicidal thoughts
or emotional crisis. It identifies warning signs, internal coping strategies, supportive
contacts, professional resources, and methods to restrict access to lethal means.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sagittal Plane} A vertical plane that divides the body or an organ into right and left sides.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salbutamol (Albuterol)} A selective beta-2 adrenergic agonist bronchodilator used for acute relief and
prevention of bronchospasm in asthma and COPD. Relaxes airway smooth muscle;
short-acting (SABA), onset 5-15 minutes, duration 4-6 hours via inhaler or nebulizer.
Adverse effects: tremor, tachycardia, palpitations, hypokalemia, and headache.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Albuterol

\medterm{Salicylate} A group of compounds that includes aspirin and has analgesic, antipyretic, and anti-inflammatory effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salicylate Toxicity} Poisoning caused by excessive aspirin or another salicylate, potentially producing vomiting,
ringing in the ears, fever, abnormal breathing, confusion, and acid-base disturbances.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Saline Infusion Sonohysterography} Ultrasound evaluation of uterus using saline contrast. Evaluates intrauterine pathology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Saliva} Secretion of salivary glands (~1-1.5 L/day). Functions: lubrication, digestion
(amylase), buffering, antimicrobial, remineralization. Xerostomia (dry mouth) increases
caries risk. Causes: medications, Sjogren syndrome, radiation, dehydration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salivary Gland} Any exocrine gland that secretes saliva into the oral cavity. The major paired glands
are the parotid, submandibular, and sublingual glands; numerous minor glands occur
throughout the oral mucosa.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salivary Gland Cancer} A rare malignancy most often arising in the parotid gland. Histologic types include
mucoepidermoid carcinoma, adenoid cystic carcinoma, and acinic cell carcinoma; facial
nerve preservation is a key surgical consideration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salivary Gland Infection} Infection of a salivary gland, usually bacterial and often involving the parotid or
submandibular gland. Painful swelling, fever, dry mouth, and purulent drainage from the
duct may occur.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salivary Gland Scintigraphy} Salivary gland function assessment using Tc-99m pertechnetate. Used for Sjogren
syndrome, obstruction, radiation sialadenitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salivary Gland Tumors} Tumors arising in the salivary glands. Many are benign, especially in the parotid gland;
pleomorphic adenoma is a common benign type. Malignant types include mucoepidermoid,
adenoid cystic, and acinic cell carcinoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salivation} The secretion of saliva by the salivary glands.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salmonella} A genus of Gram-negative, facultatively anaerobic, rod-shaped bacteria in the Enterobacteriaceae family.
It includes non-typhoidal strains that cause gastroenteritis and typhoidal strains that can
cause enteric fever.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salmonellosis} Infection with Salmonella bacteria (non-typhoidal), typically acquired from contaminated
food (eggs, poultry, meat). Presents with acute gastroenteritis: diarrhea, fever, and
abdominal cramps within 6-72 hours of ingestion. Self-limited in immunocompetent
patients. Bacteremia can occur, especially in immunocompromised.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salpingectomy} Surgical removal of a fallopian tube. It may be used to treat a tubal ectopic pregnancy,
hydrosalpinx, or other tubal disease. The approach and whether the tube is removed depend
on the clinical condition, fertility plans, and the state of the opposite tube.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salpingitis} Inflammation of the fallopian tubes, most commonly caused by ascending infection from
the lower genital tract. Salpingitis is a component of pelvic inflammatory disease and
is often bilateral.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salpingo-oophorectomy} Surgical removal of one or both fallopian tubes and ovaries. Bilateral
salpingo-oophorectomy treats and stages ovarian cancer and is used as risk-reducing
surgery in BRCA mutation carriers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salpingography} Contrast imaging of fallopian tubes. Part of hysterosalpingography.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salpingostomy (Salpingotomy)} A fertility-preserving surgical procedure for tubal ectopic pregnancy: a linear incision
on the antimesenteric border of the fallopian tube to remove the ectopic gestation,
leaving the tube in situ. Indications: unruptured ectopic, desire for future fertility,
contralateral tubal damage or absence.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Salpingotomy

\medterm{Saltatory Conduction} The rapid propagation of action potentials along myelinated nerve fibers, where the
action potential appears to "jump" from one Node of Ranvier to the next. Myelin
(produced by Schwann cells in the PNS and oligodendrocytes in the CNS) insulates the
axon, preventing ion flow across the internodal membrane.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salvage Therapy} Treatment given for cancer that has not responded to, or has returned after, an earlier
course of treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sandhoff Disease} An autosomal-recessive lysosomal storage disorder caused by deficiency of
beta-hexosaminidase A and B, leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside. It causes
progressive neurodegeneration, developmental regression, seizures, hypotonia, and
sometimes organ enlargement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sandpaper Tooth} Rough enamel surface retains plaque. May indicate early demineralization or fluorosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Saphenous Vein} A superficial vein of the lower limb that drains the foot and leg. The great and small saphenous
veins are clinically important in venous disease and may be used as graft material in selected
vascular or cardiac bypass procedures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SAPHO Syndrome} A rare inflammatory disorder involving synovitis, acne, pustulosis, hyperostosis, and osteitis. It commonly affects
the anterior chest wall, spine, or jaw and may resemble infection or malignancy. Diagnosis is clinical and treatment
may include anti-inflammatory or specialist immunomodulatory therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sarcoidosis} Sarcoidosis is multisystem granulomatous inflammation, commonly affecting lungs and lymph nodes but also skin, eyes, heart, nerves, and other organs.
It commonly affects the lungs and lymph nodes but may involve the skin, eyes, heart, nervous system, or other organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sarcoidosis Skin Manifestations} Cutaneous sarcoidosis affects 25 percent of patients with systemic sarcoidosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sarcoma} A malignant tumor arising from connective or supporting tissues, including bone, cartilage, fat,
muscle, and blood vessels. Examples include osteosarcoma, liposarcoma, and rhabdomyosarcoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sarcopenia} Age-related loss of muscle mass and function. Prevalence increases with age. Risk
factors: reduced protein intake, decreased physical activity, chronic disease.
Assessment: grip strength, gait speed, muscle mass measurement. Treatment: protein
supplementation, resistance exercise, vitamin D, hormonal therapy if deficient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sarcopenic Obesity} The coexistence of excess adiposity with reduced skeletal-muscle mass, strength, or
physical performance. It increases risks of disability, falls, metabolic disease, and
poor surgical outcomes and requires assessment beyond body-mass index alone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sarcoptes scabiei} A mite that causes scabies, a contagious skin infestation transmitted by prolonged
skin-to-skin contact. The female mite burrows into the stratum corneum, laying eggs
along the burrow, which appears as a greyish, serpiginous line. The life cycle from egg
to adult takes approximately 10-14 days.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SARS-CoV-2} A coronavirus belonging to the Coronaviridae family that causes COVID-19. SARS-CoV-2 has
a positive-sense single-stranded RNA genome and a spike glycoprotein that mediates
attachment to ACE2 receptors. The virus replicates in the cytoplasm using its own
RNA-dependent RNA polymerase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Satiety} Feeling of fullness after eating. Affected by meal composition, volume, fiber, protein.
Satiety hormones: CCK, PYY, GLP-1.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Saturated Fat} Solid at room temperature. Sources: meat, dairy, coconut oil, palm oil. Controversial
cardiovascular effects. Current guidelines limit intake.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Saturation} Occupancy of available binding, metabolic, transport, or elimination capacity such that
further increases in concentration or dose produce a nonlinear response. Saturable
metabolism can cause disproportionate rises in plasma concentration and toxicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scabies} Scabies is infestation by Sarcoptes scabiei mites, causing intense nighttime itching and a characteristic rash or burrows, with spread through close contact.
mite Sarcoptes scabiei var. hominis. The hallmark symptom is severe, generalized
pruritus that characteristically worsens at night, driven by a hypersensitivity reaction
to the mite's proteins, eggs, and feces.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scaffold} Three-dimensional structure for tissue engineering. Porous, biodegradable. Cells
populate scaffold.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scaling and Root Planing} Nonsurgical periodontal instrumentation that removes supra- and subgingival deposits and
smooths contaminated root surfaces. It reduces inflammation and pocket depth in
appropriate patients but does not regenerate lost periodontal attachment by itself.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scalp Psoriasis} Psoriasis affecting the scalp, causing sharply demarcated red plaques covered by silvery scale that may extend beyond
the hairline. It can itch, crack, or bleed and is not contagious. Treatment may include medicated shampoos, topical
corticosteroids, vitamin-D analogues, or systemic therapy for extensive disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scalpel} Surgical knife with interchangeable blades. Blade numbers indicate size and shape. \#10:
common for incisions. \#11: puncture. \#15: precise work.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scalpel Handle} Handle for holding scalpel blades. \#3 handle common.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scaphoid} Largest proximal carpal row bone on the radial wrist side. Most commonly fractured
carpal bone from falls on an outstretched hand. Unique distal blood supply makes waist
fractures prone to proximal fragment avascular necrosis. Anatomical snuffbox tenderness
is characteristic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scaphoid Fractures} Scaphoid fractures: most common carpal fracture (young males, FOOSH). Classification:
Herbert: A (incomplete), B (complete: B1 distal waist, B2 proximal waist, B3 proximal
pole, B4 distal tubercle, B5 transverse). Vascularity: retrograde from distal radius ->
proximal pole at risk for AVN.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scapula} Flat triangular bone on the posterior thorax between ribs 2-7. Key features: acromion,
coracoid process, glenoid cavity, spine, supraspinous/infraspinous fossae. Stabilized by
17 muscles and serves as rotator cuff attachment. Has 3 borders, 3 angles, and 2
surfaces. Fractures are uncommon, usually from high-energy trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scar Management} Measures used to reduce symptoms and abnormal scar formation. Options include silicone
products, pressure therapy, massage, corticosteroid injections, or other specialist treatments, depending on the scar
and its cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scarlatina} Scarlet fever, an illness caused by toxin-producing group A Streptococcus and characterized by sore throat, fever, and a fine rough-textured rash.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scarlet Fever} A toxin-mediated illness caused by Group A Streptococcus producing erythrogenic toxin.
Presents with fever, pharyngitis, and a characteristic diffuse, erythematous,
sandpaper-textured rash that blanches with pressure. Circumoral pallor, Pastia lines
(linear petechiae in skin folds), and strawberry tongue are classic findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scarring Alopecia} Permanent hair loss caused by destruction and fibrosis of the hair follicle. Causes
include lichen planopilaris, discoid lupus, central centrifugal cicatricial alopecia,
folliculitis decalvans, and severe infection; early diagnosis and biopsy can prevent
progression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scatter Correction} Correction for scattered photons. Dual energy window method.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scatter Events} Photons deflected from original path. Corrected by energy windowing and scatter
correction algorithms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scavenging System} The anesthesia gas scavenging system collects and removes waste anesthetic gases from
the operating room environment to protect staff from chronic exposure. Active scavenging
uses negative pressure from vacuum source; passive scavenging vents to exhaust through
gravity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schatzki Ring} Thin circumferential mucosal ring located at the squamocolumnar junction of the distal
esophagus at the superior margin of a hiatal hernia. The ring is composed of mucosa and
submucosa. May be asymptomatic or cause episodic dysphagia particularly to solid food
and acute food bolus impaction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schedule I} A United States controlled-substance category for drugs with high misuse potential and no accepted medical use
under federal law.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schedule II} A United States controlled-substance category for drugs with accepted medical uses but high misuse potential.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schedule III} A United States controlled-substance category for drugs with lower misuse potential than Schedule II drugs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schedule IV} A United States controlled-substance category for drugs with accepted medical uses and lower misuse potential
than Schedule III drugs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schedule V} A United States controlled-substance category for drugs with the lowest level of misuse potential among the
federal schedules.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schilling Test} A historical test of vitamin B12 absorption, formerly used to investigate pernicious
anaemia and intestinal malabsorption. It has largely been replaced by serum vitamin B12,
methylmalonic acid, homocysteine, and intrinsic-factor antibody testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schistocytes} Fragmented, helmet-shaped, or triangular red blood cells produced by mechanical shearing
as erythrocytes pass through obstructed or fibrin-laden microvessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schistosoma haematobium} A parasitic blood fluke that can cause urinary schistosomiasis, including hematuria and chronic
injury of the urinary tract.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schistosomiasis} Schistosomiasis concerns schistosomiasis; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
Schistosoma. Infection occurs when cercariae in contaminated freshwater penetrate the
skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
bilharzia, caused by blood flukes of the genus.

\medterm{Schizencephaly} A rare brain malformation characterized by a full-thickness, gray matter-lined cleft
extending from the pial surface to the lateral ventricle. Caused by early focal neuronal
migration abnormalities, often associated with prenatal vascular disruption or COL4A1
mutations. Closed-lip (Type I): the cleft walls are apposed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schizoaffective Disorder} A disorder characterized by an uninterrupted period of illness during which there is a
major mood episode concurrent with criterion A of schizophrenia (delusions,
hallucinations, disorganized speech, grossly disorganized or catatonic behavior, or
negative symptoms).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schizoid Personality Disorder} A pattern of detachment from social relationships with a restricted range of emotional
expression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schizophrenia} Schizophrenia is a chronic psychotic disorder involving delusions, hallucinations, disorganized thought or behavior, negative symptoms, and impaired functioning.
delusions, disorganized thinking) and negative symptoms (avolition, alogia, anhedonia,
blunted affect), with cognitive impairment and functional decline. Diagnosis: at least two core
symptoms for \(\geq 1\) month with continuous signs for \(\geq 6\) months.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schizotypal Personality Disorder} A pattern of social and interpersonal deficits marked by acute discomfort with and
reduced capacity for close relationships, cognitive or perceptual distortions, and
eccentricities of behavior.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schnitzler Syndrome} A rare autoinflammatory syndrome characterized by chronic urticarial rash, intermittent
fever, bone pain, arthralgia or arthritis, and elevated inflammatory markers, usually
with a monoclonal IgM protein and less commonly IgG.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{School-Based Rehabilitation} Therapy and other support services provided in a school setting to help a child participate in education and daily school activities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schwannoma} A benign peripheral nerve sheath tumor arising from Schwann cells. It presents as a
solitary, slow-growing, painless nodule along a peripheral nerve course. It can be moved
laterally but not longitudinally along the nerve. Vestibular schwannoma (acoustic
neuroma) arises from CN VIII. Diagnosis is by MRI.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sciatic Nerve} Body's largest nerve (L4-S3), formed from the lumbosacral plexus, exiting via the
greater sciatic foramen below the piriformis. Descends in the posterior thigh, dividing
into tibial and common peroneal nerves at the popliteal fossa. Innervates hamstrings and
provides lower leg/foot motor/sensory innervation via its branches.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sciatica} Sciatica is radiating leg pain or sensory symptoms along a lumbosacral nerve-root distribution, commonly from disk herniation or spinal narrowing.
through the buttock and down the posterior thigh to the foot. Most commonly caused by
lumbar disc herniation compressing the nerve root (L4-L5 or L5-S1). Other causes: spinal
stenosis, spondylolisthesis, piriformis syndrome, and epidural abscess.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scimitar Syndrome} A congenital cardiopulmonary anomaly involving partial or total anomalous pulmonary
venous return from the right lung to the inferior vena cava, often with right-lung
hypoplasia and dextroposition of the heart. It may cause recurrent respiratory
infection, pulmonary hypertension, or heart failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scintillation} The emission of light by a material after it absorbs ionizing radiation. Scintillation detectors use this light to measure radiation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scintillation Crystal} Sodium iodide doped with thallium (NaI:Tl) crystal converts gamma rays to light photons.
Coupled to photomultiplier tubes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scissors} Cutting instruments. Types: Mayo (tissue), Metzenbaum (delicate), suture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scissors (Surgical)} Cutting instruments used to divide tissue, sutures, dressings, or other materials during medical procedures.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Surgical

\medterm{Scissors (Vascular)} A surgical instrument with two blades used to cut tissue, sutures, dressings, or other materials
during vascular procedures.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Sclera} Sclera is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations. muscle attachment. White, covering approximately five-sixths of the globe. Continuous with cornea anteriorly (limbus) and dura mater posteriorly through the optic nerve sheath.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scleral Buckling} A retinal-detachment repair procedure in which a silicone band or buckle is placed around the outside of the eye to support the retinal break and reduce traction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scleritis} Scleritis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
blurred vision, and tenderness and can be associated with autoimmune disease; vision changes
or severe eye pain require urgent ophthalmic assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scleroderma} Scleroderma is an autoimmune connective-tissue disease causing skin thickening and variable fibrosis or vascular injury in the gastrointestinal tract, lungs, kidneys, or heart.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scleromyxedema} A rare, chronic, progressive mucinosis characterised by generalised papular and
sclerodermoid thickening of the skin with firm, waxy papules in a "peau d'orange"
pattern, most prominently on the face, neck, and hands.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sclerosing sialadenitis} A chronic fibrosing inflammation of the salivary glands characterized by progressive
glandular enlargement and firmness, sometimes associated with IgG4-related disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sclerosteosis} A rare inherited sclerosing bone dysplasia characterized by progressive thickening of
the skull and long bones, tall stature or syndactyly in some forms, and possible
cranial-nerve compression. Hearing loss, facial palsy, headache, and raised intracranial
pressure may occur.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scoliosis} Scoliosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
radiographs. It may be idiopathic, congenital, or associated with neuromuscular disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scotoma} A focal area of diminished or absent vision within an otherwise intact visual field,
classified by location and morphology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Screening} Testing people without symptoms to identify a disease or an increased risk of disease. The appropriate test,
age range, interval, and follow-up depend on the condition, individual risk, and current
guidance; screening benefits must be balanced against harms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Screening (Geriatric)} Detection of disease in asymptomatic elderly. Balance benefits vs. harms, life
expectancy, preferences. Examples: bone density, abdominal aortic aneurysm, colorectal
cancer (individualized), depression, falls.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Geriatric

\medterm{Screening Guidelines} Recommendations describing who should be screened for a disease, which test to use, and when to begin or repeat testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Screening Program} An organized public-health service that systematically invites a defined population to
undergo testing for an asymptomatic condition, with quality-assured follow-up,
diagnosis, treatment, and evaluation of benefits and harms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Screening Test} A test applied to people without symptoms or a known diagnosis to identify those at
increased risk of a condition. A screening result usually requires a diagnostic or
confirmatory evaluation before treatment is initiated.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Screws} Threaded devices used to hold bone fragments together or attach implants to bone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scrofula} Scrofula concerns scrofula; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
nodes, caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis or atypical mycobacteria. It presents as
chronic, painless, matted lymphadenopathy that may develop into cold abscesses with
draining sinuses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scrotal calcinosis} The deposition of calcium within the scrotal skin, presenting as multiple, firm,
whitish, painless nodules on the scrotum, which may be solitary or numerous and may
ulcerate. The exact aetiology is debated and may involve dystrophic calcification of
pre-existing epidermal inclusion cysts, dermal cysts, or eccrine duct abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scrotal Infection} Infection of the scrotal skin or underlying structures, which may cause redness, swelling, warmth, pain,
or drainage. Rapidly worsening pain, fever, discoloration, or severe swelling is an emergency
because serious soft-tissue infection must be excluded.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scrotal Pain} Pain in the scrotum caused by infection, trauma, hernia, fluid collections, varicocele, or testicular torsion.
Sudden severe pain, nausea, a high-riding testis, or abnormal testicular position requires
immediate assessment for torsion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scrotum} Scrotum concerns scrotum, a body structure, substance, or clinical concept whose meaning depends on location, function, and patient context.
spermatic cords. The scrotal wall has multiple layers reflecting the abdominal wall
layers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scurvy} Scurvy concerns scurvy; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
is essential for collagen synthesis. Vitamin C serves as a cofactor for prolyl and lysyl
hydroxylases, which hydroxylate collagen fibers to allow stable cross-linking.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seasonal Affective Disorder} A specifier of recurrent major depressive episodes that occur at a specific time of
year, most commonly winter (with remission in spring/summer). Symptoms: hypersomnia,
hyperphagia with carbohydrate craving, weight gain, fatigue, and low energy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sebaceous Gland} A small gland in the skin that secretes sebum (oil) into hair follicles to lubricate and
waterproof the skin and hair.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seborrheic Dermatitis} A chronic inflammatory skin condition affecting sebum-rich areas: scalp, face
(nasolabial folds, eyebrows), chest, and intertriginous areas. Presents with
erythematous patches with greasy, yellowish scales. Dandruff is a mild form. More common
and severe in Parkinson disease, HIV/AIDS, and Down syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seborrheic keratoses} Common, benign, epidermal skin tumors presenting as waxy, stuck-on, pigmented plaques
with a rough, verrucous surface, typically appearing on the trunk and face of
middle-aged and older adults. They are the most common benign skin neoplasm and are
caused by keratinocyte proliferation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seborrheic Keratosis} A benign epidermal tumor appearing as stuck-on, waxy, hyperpigmented plaques with a
verrucous surface. They are extremely common in older adults and increase in number with
age. Seborrheic keratoses are not premalignant.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seborrhoea} A chronic inflammatory skin condition affecting sebum-rich areas: scalp, face
(nasolabial folds, eyebrows), chest, and intertriginous areas. See Seborrheic
Dermatitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seckel Syndrome} A rare recessive primordial dwarfism syndrome featuring severe proportionate growth
restriction, microcephaly, characteristic facial features, and developmental or
intellectual impairment. Skeletal, hematological, and other congenital abnormalities may
occur.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second Malignancy} New cancer after initial cancer treatment. Risk from radiation, chemotherapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second Messenger Systems} Intracellular signaling molecules that relay extracellular signals (first messengers:
hormones, neurotransmitters, growth factors) to cellular responses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second Messengers} Intracellular signaling molecules generated in response to extracellular signals (first
messengers like hormones or neurotransmitters). cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate):
produced from ATP by adenylyl cyclase, activated by Gs-protein coupled receptors,
activates protein kinase A (PKA); degraded by phosphodiesterase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second Opinion} An independent medical assessment by another qualified clinician to help confirm or clarify a diagnosis, prognosis, or treatment plan.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second Stage of Labor} From full cervical dilatation (10 cm) to delivery of the baby. The mother feels the urge
to push as the presenting part descends. Pushing: closed-glottis (Valsalva) or
open-glottis. Duration: up to 3 hours in nulliparas with regional anesthesia (2 hours
without), and 2 hours in multiparas (1 hour without).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second Trimester} The period from 13 to 27 weeks gestational age. Fetal growth accelerates, mother begins to feel quickening (fetal movement) between 16-20 weeks. Anatomy ultrasound at 18-22 weeks screens for structural anomalies. Maternal serum screening offered. Nausea typically resolves.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second-Degree AV Block} Intermittent failure of AV conduction. Mobitz Type I (Wenckebach): progressive PR
prolongation then dropped QRS; usually benign (AV nodal, narrow QRS). Mobitz Type II:
constant PR interval with sudden dropped QRS; more serious (infranodal, wide QRS), risk
of progression to complete heart block and Stokes-Adams attacks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second-Degree Burn} Partial-thickness burn involving the epidermis and dermis: superficial partial-thickness
(blisters, moist, painful, pink) vs. deep partial-thickness (blanching, painful, may
have thrombosed capillaries). Causes: thermal (scalds, flames), chemical, electrical.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Second-Hand Smoke} Smoke inhaled from burning tobacco products or exhaled by a person who is smoking. It contains toxic
substances and increases health risks in exposed people.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Attack Rate} The proportion of susceptible contacts who develop infection after exposure to a primary
case within a defined period. It helps estimate transmissibility in households, schools,
healthcare settings, and other close-contact groups.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Hyperparathyroidism} Compensatory PTH increase in CKD from decreased phosphate excretion, low calcitriol, and
hypocalcemia. Leads to renal osteodystrophy and vascular calcification. Managed with
dietary phosphorus restriction, phosphate binders, active vitamin D, and calcimimetics.
Refractory cases may require parathyroidectomy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Immune Response} The rapid, enhanced immune response on re-exposure to an antigen, mediated by memory B
cells and memory T cells generated during the primary response. Features: shorter lag
phase, higher and faster antibody titers (predominantly high-affinity IgG), and more
effective cell-mediated responses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Immunodeficiency} Reduced immune function caused by an acquired condition or exposure rather than a primary genetic disorder. Causes
include HIV infection, cancer, malnutrition, medicines, and chronic disease; the
manifestations depend on which immune functions are impaired.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Prevention} Early detection and treatment. Examples: screening (mammography, colonoscopy, blood
pressure), self-examination.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary syphilis} The systemic dissemination stage of Treponema pallidum infection, occurring weeks to
months after the primary chancre, characterised by a generalised maculopapular rash
(including the palms and soles), mucous patches, condylomata lata, lymphadenopathy, and
systemic symptoms such as fever and malaise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Tumor} A tumor formed when cancer cells spread from the original site to another part of the body.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secretin} A peptide hormone produced by S-cells in the duodenal and jejunal mucosa in response to
acidic chyme (pH less than 4.5) entering the duodenum from the stomach.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secretion} Active movement of a substance from cells into a body fluid, lumen, or external
environment. In toxicokinetics, renal tubular or biliary secretion can contribute to
elimination of a toxin or metabolite.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secretory Diarrhea} Diarrhea caused by active secretion or reduced absorption of electrolytes and water by
the intestine. It persists despite fasting and may result from infections, medications,
bile-acid disorders, endocrine tumors, or inflammatory disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sedation Dentistry} Pharmacological techniques for anxiety management: nitrous oxide (laughing gas), oral
sedation, IV sedation, general anesthesia. Indications: dental phobia, special needs,
prolonged procedures. Monitoring required for deeper sedation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sedation Levels} A continuum ranging from minimal sedation, in which normal responses to verbal commands are preserved, to moderate or
deep sedation, in which responses and airway or breathing support may be reduced. The
level can change and requires appropriate monitoring.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sedative} A medication that reduces central nervous system activity, producing calming effects,
drowsiness, and sleep at higher doses. Sedatives are used for anxiety, procedural
sedation, insomnia, and as adjuncts in anesthesia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sedative-Hypnotic} CNS depressants. Benzodiazepines, barbiturates, Z-drugs. Overdose: respiratory
depression, coma. Treatment: supportive, flumazenil (benzodiazepines only), airway
management.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Segmental Pressures} Blood pressure measurements at different leg levels. Localizes level of obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Segmentation} A pattern of intestinal motility involving localized, rhythmic contractions of circular
muscle that segment the intestinal contents, mixing them with digestive secretions and
increasing contact with the absorptive mucosal surface. Unlike peristalsis, segmentation
does not propel contents distally.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seizure} A transient occurrence of signs and/or symptoms due to abnormal, excessive, or
synchronous neuronal activity in the brain. Provoked seizures have an identifiable acute
cause (fever, electrolyte disturbance, drug toxicity, withdrawal). Unprovoked seizures
occur without an acute trigger.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Selective Estrogen Receptor Modulator} A class of medications that bind to estrogen receptors (ER-alpha and ER-beta) with
tissue-specific agonist or antagonist activity, depending on the target tissue and
differential co-regulator recruitment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Selective Mutism} Consistent failure to speak in specific social situations where speaking is expected
despite speaking in other situations. The disturbance interferes with educational or
occupational achievement or social communication. Must last for at least one month and
is not attributable to lack of knowledge of or comfort with the spoken language.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Selenium} Trace mineral, component of antioxidant enzymes. Deficiency: Keshan disease
(cardiomyopathy), Kashin-Beck disease (osteoarthropathy). Sources: Brazil nuts, seafood,
meat.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Selenium-75} Calibration source. Half-life 119 days. Used for uniformity correction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Self-Efficacy} Patient belief in ability to manage condition. Influences adherence, outcomes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Self-Experimentation} Research in which an investigator deliberately uses an intervention or procedure on themself. It
requires careful attention to consent, safety, and scientific validity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Self-Harm} Intentional self-inflicted injury without suicidal intent, also termed non-suicidal
self-injury; common methods include cutting, burning, and self-hitting. Prevalent in
adolescents and in borderline personality disorder, depression, eating disorders, and
trauma, serving affect regulation or self-punishment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Self-Management} Actions a person takes to monitor and manage a health condition, including treatment use, symptom monitoring, and lifestyle measures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Semen} The fluid ejaculated from the male reproductive tract, containing sperm (produced in the
testes) suspended in seminal plasma (produced by the seminal vesicles, prostate, and
bulbourethral glands). Semen provides nutrients (fructose, amino acids), protective
agents (zinc, antioxidants), and a medium for sperm transport.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Semen Analysis} Laboratory assessment of ejaculate volume, sperm concentration, total count, motility,
morphology, vitality, and other characteristics. It is a key initial test in male-factor
infertility and should usually be repeated because results vary biologically.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Semicircular Canals} Three fluid-filled (endolymph) loops in each inner ear, oriented approximately
orthogonally to detect angular (rotational) acceleration in three planes: horizontal
(lateral), anterior (superior), and posterior. Each has an ampulla with a crista
containing hair cells embedded in a gelatinous cupula.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seminal Vesicle} One of a paired set of male accessory reproductive glands behind the urinary bladder.
Its fructose-rich secretion contributes substantially to semen and joins the vas
deferens through the ejaculatory duct.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seminoma} A malignant germ-cell tumor of the testis, typically presenting as a painless testicular
mass and highly sensitive to radiation and chemotherapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Senna} A stimulant laxative containing anthraquinone glycosides (sennosides) that are activated
by colonic bacteria to stimulate colonic motility and fluid secretion. Onset: 6-12
hours; used for constipation and bowel preparation. Common side effects: abdominal
cramps, nausea; chronic use may cause melanosis coli and electrolyte disturbances.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sensitivity} The proportion of people who truly have a disease who obtain a positive test result. A
highly sensitive test has few false-negative results and is useful when a negative
result is intended to help rule out disease in an appropriate clinical setting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sensorineural Hearing Loss} Hearing loss from pathology of the cochlea or auditory (eighth cranial) nerve,
characterized on pure-tone audiometry by elevated bone conduction thresholds and no
air-bone gap.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Speech Audiometry} Hearing testing that measures detection or recognition of speech at different loudness levels. It commonly includes
speech reception threshold and word-recognition testing, complementing pure-tone audiometry.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
Speech testing; speech audiometric testing.

\medterm{Sensorium} The state or level of consciousness and awareness of one's surroundings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sensory Level} A sharply demarcated horizontal boundary below which sensation is altered on the trunk
or limbs. A sensory level strongly suggests a spinal-cord lesion, although its
localization requires correlation with motor, reflex, and autonomic findings.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sensory Nerves} Nerves that transmit sensory information from peripheral receptors to the central
nervous system. Sensory (afferent) neurons have their cell bodies in the dorsal root
ganglia (spinal nerves) or cranial nerve ganglia (cranial nerves).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sentinel Event} Unexpected occurrence involving death or serious physical/psychological injury. Requires
root cause analysis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sentinel Injury} A minor but potentially significant injury in a nonmobile infant, such as unexplained
bruising, oral injury, or a fracture, that may precede more severe abuse. It warrants
careful history, complete examination, safeguarding assessment, and documentation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sentinel Lymph Node} First lymph node draining tumor site. Biopsy assesses nodal spread.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sentinel Lymph-Node Biopsy} Surgical removal and pathologic examination of the first draining lymph node or nodes
from a primary skin cancer. It is used for selected melanomas and other tumors to assess
regional metastatic risk and guide staging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sentinel Surveillance} Ongoing collection of health data from selected reporting sites, providers, or
populations chosen to represent trends rather than enumerate every event. It can provide
early warning and monitor changes in disease activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Separation Anxiety Disorder} Developmentally inappropriate and excessive fear or anxiety concerning separation from
attachment figures. The individual may have recurrent excessive distress when
anticipating or experiencing separation from home or major attachment figures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Separation of the iris} Disinsertion or dialysis of the iris root from its attachment at the ciliary body,
typically caused by blunt ocular trauma. It appears as a peripheral iridodialysis
visible on slit-lamp examination and may cause monocular double vision, glare, or
secondary glaucoma if extensive.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sepsis} Sepsis is life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by a dysregulated response to infection, with possible shock, respiratory failure, kidney injury, coagulation abnormalities, or altered consciousness.
Sepsis-3 definition: infection + SOFA score increase >= 2. Septic shock: sepsis with
vasopressor requirement and lactate >2 mmol/L.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sepsis Syndrome} The spectrum of the host's dysregulated response to infection: sepsis (infection + organ
dysfunction, SOFA increase >=2), septic shock (sepsis + hypotension requiring
vasopressors with lactate >2 mmol/L despite adequate fluid), and severe organ
dysfunction/multi-organ failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septal Hematoma} Accumulation of blood between the perichondrium and the septal cartilage of the nose,
typically following nasal trauma (nasal fracture). Presents with nasal obstruction,
pain, and a bluish, boggy swelling of the nasal septum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septic Arthritis} Joint infection, usually bacterial (Staph, Strept, Gonorrhea). Emergency: fever, severe
joint pain, swelling, inability to bear weight. Diagnosis: joint aspiration (WBC
>50,000, positive culture). Treatment: antibiotics, joint drainage (arthroscopy/open).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septic Embolism} Septic embolism occurs when infected material, typically thrombi containing bacteria,
embolizes through the bloodstream to distant sites, causing metastatic infection. The
most common source is infective endocarditis, where infected vegetations on heart valves
break off and lodge in peripheral vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septic Shock} A life-threatening form of sepsis in which infection-related circulatory and cellular dysfunction causes
persistent inadequate tissue perfusion, often requiring vasopressor support despite fluid
resuscitation. It can cause multiorgan failure and requires urgent treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septicaemia} An older term often used for sepsis, a life-threatening condition in which a dysregulated response to infection causes organ dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septicemia} Septicemia is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
generally favors bacteremia or sepsis, depending on the condition described.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septo-Optic Dysplasia} A congenital disorder characterized by variable combinations of optic-nerve hypoplasia,
midline brain abnormalities, and pituitary hormone deficiencies. It may cause visual
impairment, nystagmus, developmental differences, seizures, and growth or endocrine
problems.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septoplasty} Septoplasty is a medical term used to describe a clinical, biological, or healthcare concept; its precise interpretation depends on the definition, associated findings, and context of use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sequelae} Conditions resulting from previous disease. Examples: post-polio syndrome, stroke
sequelae.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sequential Therapy} Treatments given in sequence. Used when combination not suitable.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serodiagnostic} Relating to diagnosis by detecting antibodies or other markers in blood serum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serology} The laboratory evaluation of serum for antibodies, antigens, or other immune markers.
Results may indicate current infection, previous exposure, vaccination, autoimmune
reactivity, or passive antibody transfer and must be interpreted with timing and
clinical context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seronegative Arthritis} Arthritis with negative rheumatoid factor and anti-CCP. Includes spondyloarthropathies,
psoriatic arthritis, reactive arthritis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serotonin Syndrome} A potentially life-threatening condition caused by excessive serotonergic activity in
the central and peripheral nervous systems, typically resulting from drug interactions
or overdose involving serotonergic medications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serotonin-Norepinephrine Reuptake Inhibitors} Antidepressants that block the reuptake of both serotonin and norepinephrine. They
include venlafaxine, duloxetine, desvenlafaxine, and levomilnacipran. SNRIs are used for
major depressive disorder, generalized anxiety disorder, diabetic neuropathy,
fibromyalgia, and chronic pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serotonin-Norepinephrine-Dopamine Reuptake Inhibitors} A class of antidepressants that inhibit reuptake of serotonin, norepinephrine, and
dopamine. The primary agent is bupropion, which also acts as a norepinephrine-dopamine
reuptake inhibitor. It is used for depression, smoking cessation, and as augmentation.
It lacks sexual side effects and weight gain associated with SSRIs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serotype} A group within a species of microorganisms (or viruses) distinguished by antigenic
differences, usually surface antigens (capsular polysaccharides, O/H antigens, viral
surface proteins).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serpentine supravenous hyperpigmentation} A dermatological finding characterized by dark, wavy, serpiginous hyperpigmentation
along the course of superficial veins, most commonly on the upper chest and neck.
Associated with venous stasis, thrombophlebitis, and certain malignancies. The
pigmentation results from hemosiderin deposition in the perivenous dermis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serrated Pathway} Alternative molecular pathway to colorectal carcinogenesis accounting for fifteen to
thirty percent of cases. It involves serrated polyps as precursors including sessile
serrated lesions with dysplasia. Molecular features include CpG island methylator
phenotype, BRAF mutations, and microsatellite instability from MLH1 silencing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serratus Anterior Muscle} Fan-shaped muscle from ribs 1-8/9 to the medial scapular border. Innervated by long
thoracic nerve (C5-C7). Protracts scapula, upwardly rotates glenoid for overhead
elevation, stabilizes against thorax. Long thoracic nerve injury causes medial scapular
winging, especially visible during wall push-ups. Commonly injured in breast surgery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sertoli-cell tumor} A sex-cord stromal tumor that may produce androgens or estrogens and can cause endocrine
manifestations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serum Concentration} The concentration of a substance measured in the liquid component of clotted blood.
Interpretation depends on sampling time, exposure history, distribution, metabolism,
elimination, and the relationship between concentration and toxicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serum Osmolal Gap} The difference between measured serum osmolality and calculated osmolality. An increased
gap suggests unmeasured osmoles, including toxic alcohols, but interpretation must
consider timing, renal function, lactate, ketones, and laboratory variation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serum Protein Electrophoresis} Separation of serum proteins by electrophoresis into 5 fractions: albumin, alpha-1,
alpha-2, beta, and gamma. Monoclonal gammopathy: M-spike in gamma region (multiple
myeloma, MGUS, Waldenstrom macroglobulinemia). Immunofixation: identifies heavy chain
(IgG, IgA, IgM, IgD, IgE) and light chain (kappa, lambda) isotype.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serum Sickness} Type III hypersensitivity reaction. Fever, rash, arthritis, proteinuria. Caused by drugs
(penicillin, cephalosporins), antivenoms. Treatment: stop offending agent,
antihistamines, corticosteroids.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sever Disease} Calcaneal apophysitis in children/adolescents. Heel pain, worse with activity.
Treatment: rest, heel cups, stretching.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome} A severe respiratory illness caused by SARS coronavirus, characterized by fever, dry
cough, dyspnea, and viral pneumonia that may progress to respiratory failure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Severe Combined Immunodeficiency} Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) represents a group of genetic disorders
characterized by profoundly defective T cell development and function, with variable B
and NK cell involvement, resulting in severe susceptibility to life-threatening
infections in infancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sevoflurane} A volatile inhaled anesthetic with relatively low blood-gas solubility, permitting rapid
changes in anesthetic depth and recovery. It can cause dose-dependent cardiovascular and
respiratory depression and is a recognized trigger of malignant hyperthermia in
susceptible patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sexual Assault} A broad legal category of any non-consensual sexual contact or penetration, encompassing
rape, indecent assault, and drug-facilitated assault. Examination documents general and
anogenital injuries, collects biological and trace samples, and screens for
drug-facilitated assault by extended-window testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sexual Dysfunction} Persistent difficulty in sexual activity causing distress. In women: low sexual desire
(hypoactive), arousal disorder, orgasmic disorder (anorgasmia), and genito-pelvic
pain/penetration disorder (dyspareunia, vaginismus). In men, it may involve
erectile dysfunction, delayed or premature ejaculation, or reduced sexual desire.
Causes include medical and psychological conditions, relationship factors, and
adverse effects of substances or medicines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sexual Intercourse (Coitus)} The insertion of a penis into a partner's body for sexual pleasure, reproduction, or
both. Most commonly vaginal intercourse (penile-vaginal penetration) but may also
include anal intercourse. Sexual intercourse is a key component of human pair bonding
and reproduction.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Coitus

\medterm{Sexual Masochism Disorder} A paraphilic disorder characterized by recurrent, intense sexual arousal from the act of
being humiliated, beaten, bound, or otherwise made to suffer, lasting \(\geq 6\) months and
causing distress or impairment or involving acting on these urges.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sexual Rehabilitation} Address sexual function after injury, illness. Counseling, devices, medications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sexual Sadism Disorder} A paraphilic disorder characterized by recurrent, intense sexual arousal from the
physical or psychological suffering of another person, lasting \(\geq 6\) months and causing
distress or impairment or involving acting on these urges with a nonconsenting person.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sexually Transmitted Infection} STI: infection transmitted via sexual contact. Examples: syphilis, gonorrhea, chlamydia,
HIV.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SGLT2 Inhibitor} A drug that reduces sodium-glucose cotransport in the proximal renal tubule, increasing
urinary glucose and sodium excretion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shadowing Artifact} Ultrasound artifact from attenuating structures. Enhanced by harmonic imaging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shaken Baby Syndrome} A form of abusive head trauma caused by violent shaking, typically in infants under 1
year. The triad: subdural hemorrhage, retinal hemorrhages, and encephalopathy. May
present with irritability, lethargy, vomiting, seizures, and apnea. CT shows subdural
hematoma; MRI shows diffuse axonal injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shared Decision-Making} A collaborative process in which clinicians and patients combine best available evidence
with patient values, preferences, goals, and circumstances to choose among reasonable
options.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sharp Force Trauma} Injury produced by an edged or pointed instrument that cuts or penetrates the skin,
divided into incised wounds (longer than deep, clean margins) and stab wounds (deeper
than wide). Wound length, depth, angle, hesitation marks, and defense injuries infer
weapon type and manner.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sharps Container} Puncture-resistant container for used needles and sharp instruments. Red, labeled
biohazard.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sharps Injury} A percutaneous or mucocutaneous injury caused by a needle or other sharp instrument that
may expose a person to blood or body fluids. Immediate wound care, incident reporting,
risk assessment, and pathogen-specific post-exposure management are required.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shave Biopsy} Removal of a raised skin lesion by horizontal slicing with a scalpel at the level of the
dermis; used for exophytic lesions such as seborrheic keratoses and superficial basal
cell carcinomas.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sheehan Syndrome} Postpartum pituitary necrosis resulting from severe hemorrhage and hypotension during
delivery. The pituitary gland is enlarged during pregnancy and particularly vulnerable
to ischemic injury from blood loss. Presentation varies from fatigue and failure to
lactate to panhypopituitarism. Growth hormone and prolactin deficiency occur first.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sheehan Syndrome (Postpartum Pituitary Necrosis)} Ischemic necrosis of the anterior pituitary gland due to severe postpartum hemorrhage
(PPH) and hypotension. The pituitary enlarges during pregnancy and is vulnerable to
ischemia from hypotensive episodes.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Postpartum Pituitary Necrosis

\medterm{Shielding} Lead, tungsten, acrylic shielding for radiation protection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shigellosis} Shigellosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations. countries). Presents with fever, abdominal cramps, tenesmus, and frequent bloody, mucoid stools. Highly contagious with low infectious dose.
\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shingles} Shingles is reactivation of varicella-zoster virus in a sensory nerve ganglion, producing painful grouped vesicles in a dermatomal pattern and possible persistent neuralgia.
causing a painful, unilateral vesicular rash in a dermatomal distribution ('belt-like'),
often preceded by burning pain. Risk increases with age and immunosuppression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shirodkar Cerclage} A transvaginal cervical cerclage technique that involves dissection of the bladder and
rectum to place the suture (Mersilene tape) as high as possible at the level of the
internal os. Technically more difficult than McDonald cerclage with higher risk of
hemorrhage and bladder injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shivering} Involuntary, rhythmic skeletal muscle contraction generating heat in response to cold
exposure or fever, a key thermoregulatory response under hypothalamic (preoptic area)
control. Shivering can increase heat production 3-5 fold.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shock} Shock is inadequate tissue perfusion and oxygen delivery, caused by hypovolemia, pump failure, obstruction, or abnormal vascular tone and leading to cellular and organ injury.
failure, and severe loss of vascular tone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shock Wave Lithotripsy} Non-invasive procedure using extracorporeal shock waves to fragment renal and ureteral
stones. The patient is positioned over a water cushion coupling the shock wave generator
to the body. Shock waves are focused on the stone under fluoroscopic or ultrasound
guidance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shone Complex} A group of congenital left-sided obstructive heart lesions, classically including
supravalvular mitral ring, parachute mitral valve, subaortic stenosis, and coarctation
of the aorta. The combination and severity vary and may lead to heart failure or
systemic outflow obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Short Bowel Syndrome} Clinical condition resulting from surgical resection or functional loss of a significant
portion of the small intestine leading to inadequate absorption of nutrients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shortness of Breath} An uncomfortable awareness of difficult or insufficient breathing. See Dyspnea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shoulder Dislocation} Complete displacement of the upper arm bone from the shoulder socket, usually caused by a fall, collision,
or forceful twisting. It causes severe pain, deformity, and inability to move the arm; urgent reduction
and assessment for associated fractures or nerve and blood-vessel injury are required.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shoulder Dystocia} An obstetric emergency where the fetal head delivers but the anterior shoulder is
impacted behind the maternal symphysis pubis. Defined as head-to-body delivery time
exceeding 60 seconds or need for ancillary maneuvers. Risk factors include macrosomia,
diabetes, prior shoulder dystocia, and operative vaginal delivery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shoulder Impingement} Pain associated with movement of the shoulder, often involving the rotator-cuff tendons or
subacromial tissues. Symptoms include pain with overhead activity and sometimes weakness;
assessment and treatment are individualized and may include exercise-based rehabilitation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shoulder Instability} Excessive or recurrent movement of the shoulder joint caused by injury, laxity, or altered
joint anatomy. Associated lesions may include a Bankart tear, Hill-Sachs lesion, or injury to the joint capsule or
ligaments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shoulder Pain} Pain in or around the shoulder caused by injury, tendon or bursa disorders, arthritis, nerve disease, or
referred pain. Sudden weakness, deformity, chest symptoms, fever, or pain after major trauma
requires prompt evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shunt} Tube diverting fluid. Types: ventriculoperitoneal (CSF), ventriculoatrial,
lumboperitoneal, ventriculopleural. Indications: hydrocephalus. Complications:
obstruction, infection, overdrainage, migration. Valves regulate pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shunt (Vascular)} A surgically created or implanted pathway that redirects blood or another fluid from one vessel or body space to another.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Shwachman Syndrome} An inherited multisystem disorder usually involving exocrine pancreatic insufficiency,
bone-marrow dysfunction, and skeletal abnormalities. Children may have steatorrhea, poor
growth, recurrent infections, neutropenia, anemia, or increased risk of myeloid
malignancy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SIADH} Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone secretion characterized by hyponatremia
(dilutional) with inappropriately concentrated urine (>100 mOsm/kg) and elevated urine
sodium (>40 mEq/L). Euvolemic hyponatremia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SIADH (Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone Secretion)} Excessive vasopressin activity causing impaired free-water excretion, euvolemic
hyponatremia, and inappropriately concentrated urine after exclusion of other causes.
Treatment depends on severity and cause and may include fluid restriction or carefully
monitored hypertonic saline for severe symptomatic cases.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone Secretion

\medterm{Sialadenitis} Inflammation of salivary gland. Acute (bacterial) or chronic (recurrent). Treatment:
antibiotics, hydration, gland massage, surgery if chronic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sialography} Contrast imaging of salivary ducts. Evaluates obstruction, stricture, tumors. Rarely
performed now.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sialolithiasis} Formation of a stone in a salivary gland or its duct, most often the submandibular gland. It may cause recurrent
swelling and pain, especially during meals, and can lead to infection or obstruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sialorrhea} Excessive salivation. Neurological causes, medications, dental causes. Treatment:
hydration, glycopyrrolate, botulinum toxin, surgery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sialorrhoea} Excessive saliva production or inability to clear saliva, resulting in drooling. Causes include medicines,
oral disease, and neurologic or swallowing disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sialuria} A rare inherited disorder of sialic-acid metabolism caused by abnormal regulation of the
GNE enzyme. Excess free sialic acid accumulates in cells and urine and may cause
developmental delay, coarse facial features, hepatosplenomegaly, and variable
intellectual disability.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sicca Syndrome} Dry eyes and dry mouth due to decreased tear and saliva production. Primary: Sjogren
syndrome. Secondary: associated with RA, SLE, scleroderma, graft-versus-host disease,
hepatitis C, sarcoidosis, HIV, medications (anticholinergics, antidepressants).
Diagnosis: Schirmer test (<5 mm/5 min), rose bengal staining, salivary gland biopsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sick Sinus Syndrome} Sinus node dysfunction causing a spectrum of arrhythmias: sinus bradycardia, sinus
arrest, sinoatrial block, and tachycardia-bradycardia syndrome. Most common in elderly
patients. Presents with syncope, fatigue, palpitations, and exercise intolerance.
Diagnosis: ECG, Holter monitor (pauses >3 seconds).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sickle Cell Anemia} The most severe form of sickle cell disease (HbSS), causing chronic hemolytic anemia and
vaso-occlusive crises. Features: jaundice, splenomegaly (infancy), pain crises, acute
chest syndrome, stroke, and increased infection risk from functional asplenia.
Diagnosis: hemoglobin electrophoresis showing HbS.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sickle Cell Crisis} An acute clinical complication of sickle cell disease, most commonly a vaso-occlusive
episode producing ischemic pain. Other acute crises include acute chest syndrome,
splenic or hepatic sequestration, and aplastic crisis; treatment depends on the specific
complication and severity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sickle Cell Dactylitis} Painful swelling of the hands or feet caused by blockage of small blood vessels during a sickle-cell
episode, especially in infants and young children. It may be an early sign of sickle-cell disease
and requires appropriate clinical evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sickle Cell Disease} Autosomal recessive hemoglobinopathy (HbS). Glutamic acid → valine at position 6 of
beta-globin. Homozygous (HbSS) most severe. Sickling under hypoxia causes
vaso-occlusion, hemolysis. Symptoms: pain crises, acute chest syndrome, stroke, splenic
sequestration, aplastic crisis (parvovirus B19).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sickle Cell Trait} An inherited carrier state in which a person has one hemoglobin S allele and one normal
beta-globin allele, usually producing both hemoglobin A and hemoglobin S. Most people are
asymptomatic, but complications can occur in severe physiologic conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Side Effect} An unintended pharmacological effect of a drug occurring at therapeutic doses, usually
predictable from the drug's mechanism of action (a Type A reaction).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Siderosis} Benign pneumoconiosis from iron dust, seen in welders. Causes small opacities on CXR but
minimal impairment. Reversible with removal of exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SIDS} Sudden unexpected death of an apparently healthy infant during sleep that remains unexplained after investigation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sievert (Sv)} The SI unit used for equivalent and effective dose, incorporating radiation type and,
for effective dose, tissue sensitivity. It is used to estimate stochastic radiation risk
rather than simply physical energy deposition.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Sv

\medterm{Sigmoid Colon} S-shaped segment from descending colon to rectum in the left iliac fossa, approximately
40-50 cm, with complete mesentery (intraperitoneal, mobile). Most common diverticular
disease site from narrow lumen and high pressure. Common volvulus site, particularly in
elderly/institutionalized patients. Second most common colorectal cancer site.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sigmoid volvulus} Axial twisting of the sigmoid colon around its mesentery, the most common cause of
colonic volvulus, typically occurring in elderly, institutionalized, or neuropsychiatric
patients with chronic constipation and a redundant sigmoid. Presents with acute large
bowel obstruction, abdominal distension, and absent bowel sounds.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sign} Objective evidence of disease. Examples: rash, murmur, edema. Observed by examiner.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Signal Transduction} The process by which an extracellular signal (hormone, neurotransmitter, growth factor,
drug) binding to a cell-surface or intracellular receptor is converted into a specific
intracellular response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Signal-to-Noise Ratio} Ratio of true counts to statistical noise. Improved by longer acquisition, higher
activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Signet Ring Cell Carcinoma} A poorly differentiated adenocarcinoma in which intracellular mucin displaces the
nucleus to the periphery, most often of the stomach or colon; associated with an
aggressive course and diffuse growth pattern.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Silicone oil hyperoleon} Emulsification of silicone oil tamponade used in vitreoretinal surgery, where the oil
breaks into tiny droplets that migrate into the anterior chamber or trabecular meshwork.
It can cause elevated intraocular pressure, band keratopathy, and chronic inflammation
requiring oil removal and anterior chamber washout.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Silicosis} Silicosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
