mining, quarrying, construction, or stone-cutting work. It causes progressive
breathlessness and increases the risk of tuberculosis, chronic airflow limitation, and
lung cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Silver Diamine Fluoride} Liquid that arrests caries and stains cavity black. Used in young children, special
needs patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinding-Larsen-Johansson Syndrome} Traction apophysitis of inferior patellar pole in adolescents. Similar to
Osgood-Schlatter but at knee. Treatment: rest, activity modification.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{single nucleotide polymorphism} Single base pair variation occurring in >1\% of population. Most common genetic
variation. Used in GWAS, forensics, pharmacogenetics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Single Photon Emission Computed Tomography} Nuclear medicine imaging using gamma-emitting isotopes (technetium-99m most common).
Rotating gamma camera acquires images. Applications: myocardial perfusion, bone scans,
thyroid imaging, renal scans, brain perfusion. Lower resolution than PET but more widely
available and cost-effective.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Single Ventricle} A group of congenital heart defects where one functional ventricle supports both the
systemic and pulmonary circulations. Includes tricuspid atresia, mitral atresia, double
inlet left ventricle, hypoplastic left heart syndrome, and unbalanced AV canal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Single-Dose Toxicity} Harm caused by one exposure to a substance or medicine, assessed according to dose, route, timing,
and the exposed person's characteristics.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinoatrial Node} The heart's natural pacemaker, located at the junction of the superior vena cava and
right atrium. It consists of specialized cells that spontaneously depolarize at a rate
of 60-100 beats per minute, setting the normal sinus rhythm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinus Lift} Procedure to increase bone height in posterior maxilla. Subperiosteal or transalveolar
approach. Graft material placed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinus Pain} Pressure or pain around the forehead, cheeks, nose, or eyes caused by sinus inflammation, infection, migraine,
or other conditions. Severe headache, eye swelling, vision change, high fever, or neurologic
symptoms requires urgent assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinus Rhomboidalis} A diamond-shaped depression in the floor of the fourth ventricle of the brain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinuses} Air-filled cavities within the skull bones lined by respiratory mucosa, connected to the
nasal cavity via ostia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinusitis} Sinusitis is inflammation or infection of the paranasal sinuses causing congestion, facial pressure, nasal discharge, reduced smell, cough, or headache.
resolves in <10 days) or bacterial (symptoms >10 days, double worsening, or severe:
Streptococcus pneumoniae, Haemophilus influenzae, Moraxella catarrhalis). Chronic
sinusitis: symptoms >12 weeks with radiographic evidence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinusitis and Sinus Problems} Inflammation or infection of the paranasal sinuses that may cause nasal congestion, discharge,
facial pressure, headache, or reduced smell. Symptoms may also arise from other nasal or
facial conditions and require assessment when persistent or severe.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinusoidal Fetal Heart Rate Pattern} A rare, ominous FHR pattern characterized by a smooth, sine-wave-like undulation with a
fixed frequency (2-5 cycles/minute) and absent variability. Strongly associated with
severe fetal anemia (Rh isoimmunization, fetomaternal hemorrhage, parvovirus B19), fetal
hypoxia, and fetal acidosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sirenomelia} A rare congenital malformation in which the lower limbs are partially or completely
fused. It is usually associated with severe abnormalities of the genitourinary,
gastrointestinal, vascular, and spinal systems and is often fatal in the neonatal
period.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sister Mary Joseph's nodule} A firm, hard nodule in the umbilicus resulting from metastatic malignancy, most commonly
from intra-abdominal or pelvic cancers including gastric, ovarian, and colorectal
carcinoma. It indicates advanced, disseminated disease with a poor prognosis. Diagnosis
is by fine needle aspiration biopsy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sitosterolemia} An autosomal-recessive disorder of plant-sterol transport caused by ABCG5 or ABCG8
variants. It can produce premature atherosclerosis, xanthomas, hemolytic anemia,
macrothrombocytopenia, and elevated plasma plant sterols.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Situs Inversus} A congenital condition in which the thoracic and abdominal organs are reversed or
mirrored from their normal positions. Complete situs inversus (situs inversus totalis)
involves all organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Situs inversus totalis} A congenital condition in which the major visceral organs are mirrored from their normal
positions, with the liver on the left, heart on the right, and other viscera similarly
reversed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sjogren Syndrome} Primary Sjogren syndrome (pSS) is a chronic autoimmune exocrinopathy characterized by
lymphocytic infiltration of lacrimal and salivary glands causing dry eyes
(keratoconjunctivitis sicca) and dry mouth (xerostomia), with female predominance (9:1)
and peak onset 40-60. Secondary SS occurs with RA, SLE, SSc.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sjögren Syndrome} A systemic autoimmune disease characterized by lymphocytic destruction and dysfunction
of lacrimal and salivary glands, producing dry eyes and dry mouth. It may involve
multiple organs and increases the risk of salivary-gland lymphoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sjögren's syndrome} A chronic autoimmune disorder characterized by lymphocytic infiltration and destruction
of exocrine glands, primarily the salivary and lacrimal glands, resulting in dry eyes
(keratoconjunctivitis sicca) and dry mouth (xerostomia).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skeletal Muscle} Voluntary, striated muscle attached to bones by tendons or directly by connective
tissue. Its contraction produces movement, maintains posture, and generates heat.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skeletal Muscle Relaxant} A medication that reduces muscle spasm, spasticity, and associated pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skeletal Survey} A series of radiographs obtained when child abuse is suspected. Includes AP and lateral
views of the skull, chest (including oblique views), abdomen, spine, and bilateral upper
and lower extremities. Identifies fractures of varying ages, which is highly suggestive
of non-accidental trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skeletonization} Final stage of postmortem decomposition where soft tissues, organs, and skin have
completely decayed or been scavenged, leaving only clean osseous skeletal remains.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skilled Nursing Facility} Inpatient facility providing skilled nursing and therapy. For recovery after
hospitalization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin} The largest organ of the body (approximately 1.5--2 m2), forming a protective barrier
against physical injury, pathogens, UV radiation, and water loss.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Absorption} Movement of a substance through the skin into underlying tissues or the bloodstream. Absorption depends
on the substance, skin condition, contact time, and exposed area.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Aging} Skin aging has intrinsic (chronological) and extrinsic (photoaging) components. Photoaging causes
wrinkle formation, telangiectasias, lentigines, leather-like texture, and actinic
keratoses. Intrinsic aging causes thinning, wrinkling, and delayed wound healing.
Prevention includes sun protection (broad-spectrum SPF 30 or greater daily).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Aging and Appearance} Skin aging is a complex biological process influenced by both intrinsic factors, such as
genetics and natural aging, and extrinsic factors, particularly ultraviolet radiation
exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Allergies and Contact Dermatitis} Inflammatory skin reactions triggered by exposure to allergens or irritants that come
into direct contact with the skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Allergy Testing} Skin allergy testing includes prick testing (immediate hypersensitivity, IgE-mediated) and intradermal testing (more
sensitive, used for drug and venom allergy). Prick testing introduces allergen extract
into the epidermis; wheal and flare reaction at 15 minutes indicates sensitization.
Intradermal testing injects dilute allergen into the dermis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin and Aging} Skin aging involves intrinsic and extrinsic changes. Intrinsic aging causes thinning,
wrinkling, and fragility. Extrinsic (photo)aging causes wrinkles, telangiectasias,
lentigines, and actinic keratoses. Collagen loss, elastin degradation, and decreased
cell turnover contribute to aging skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin and Environment} Environmental factors significantly affect skin health. Pollution causes oxidative
stress, inflammation, and accelerated aging. Particulate matter penetrates skin,
generating free radicals. Blue light from screens causes hyperpigmentation, particularly
in darker skin tones. Indoor heating and air conditioning cause dryness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Appendages} Skin structures include hair follicles, sebaceous glands, eccrine sweat glands, and apocrine sweat glands. Hair
follicles cycle through anagen (growth), catagen (transition), and telogen (resting)
phases. Sebaceous glands produce sebum, an oily substance lubricating skin and hair.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Barrier Function} The skin barrier prevents water loss and protects against environmental insults. The
stratum corneum acts as a physical barrier, while the acid mantle (pH 4 to 6) provides
chemical defense. Lipids (ceramides, cholesterol, free fatty acids) in the intercellular
space form a waterproof barrier.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Biopsy Methods} Skin biopsy methods vary by lesion characteristics. Punch biopsy (2 to 6 mm circular
blade) provides full-thickness specimen for most diagnoses. Shave biopsy (razor or
scalpel) removes superficial lesions; deep shave (saucerization) includes subcutaneous
fat for suspected melanoma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Biopsy Techniques} Skin biopsy is selected according to the suspected diagnosis and lesion depth. Punch
biopsy samples full-thickness skin; shave biopsy samples superficial lesions; excisional
biopsy removes an entire small lesion; and incisional biopsy samples part of a large
lesion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Cancer} Most common cancer. Types: basal cell, squamous cell, melanoma. Prevention: sun
protection. Early detection critical.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Cancer Epidemiology} Skin cancers are common and include basal-cell carcinoma, squamous-cell carcinoma, and
melanoma. Incidence and mortality vary by tumor type, geography, skin phenotype, ultraviolet
exposure, immune status, and access to diagnosis and treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Cancer Prevention} Prevention focuses on reducing ultraviolet exposure through shade, protective clothing,
hats, sunglasses, and appropriately used broad-spectrum sunscreen. Avoiding tanning beds also reduces ultraviolet
exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Cancer Screening} Skin cancer screening uses the ABCDE criteria for melanoma, the ugly duckling sign, and
total body skin examination. Dermoscopy (dermatoscopy) magnifies skin lesions revealing
structures invisible to the naked eye, improving diagnostic accuracy. Skin
self-examination teaches patients to monitor moles for changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Cancer Staging} Skin cancer staging guides treatment and prognosis. Melanoma: Breslow thickness (most
important prognostic factor), ulceration, mitotic rate, sentinel lymph node status (AJCC
8th edition).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Cancer Surgery} Skin cancer surgical techniques maximize cure while minimizing morbidity. Wide local
excision with appropriate margins (BCC: 4 mm, SCC: 4 to 6 mm, melanoma: based on Breslow
thickness). Mohs micrographic surgery provides 99 percent cure rate for BCC and 97
percent for SCC with tissue preservation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Flaps} Skin flaps transfer tissue with its blood supply. Rotation flaps rotate adjacent tissue
into defect. Advancement flaps slide tissue forward. Transposition flaps move tissue
across intervening skin. Random pattern flaps rely on dermal plexus; axial pattern flaps
have named vascular pedicle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Graft} Transfer of skin from a donor site to a recipient site to cover a wound. Split-thickness
skin graft (STSG): epidermis + partial dermis (0.008-0.018 inches), harvested with
dermatome, donor site heals spontaneously (2-3 weeks). Full-thickness skin graft (FTSG):
epidermis + full dermis, donor site requires closure, better cosmetic outcome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Immune System} The skin immune system (SALT: skin-associated lymphoid tissue) provides immunological
surveillance. Langerhans cells (dendritic cells in epidermis) capture antigens and
present them to T cells. Dermal dendritic cells, mast cells, and resident T cells form
an innate and adaptive immune network.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Infection} Infection of the skin or underlying soft tissue, commonly caused by bacteria, fungi, or viruses. Redness, warmth,
swelling, pain, drainage, or fever may occur; rapidly spreading redness, severe pain, blisters, or systemic
illness requires prompt medical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Integrity} Localized damage to the skin and/or underlying soft tissue caused by prolonged pressure,
shear, or friction, most commonly over bony prominences. See pressure injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Irritation} Inflammation or discomfort caused by direct contact with an irritant, often producing redness, burning,
itching, dryness, or scaling.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Microbiome} The skin microbiome is the community of microorganisms (bacteria, fungi, viruses)
residing on the skin. Staphylococcus aureus colonization is increased in atopic
dermatitis and psoriasis. Cutibacterium acnes plays a role in acne. Diversity is
decreased in diseased skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin of Color} Skin of color (Fitzpatrick IV to VI) has unique dermatologic considerations. It is more
prone to hyperpigmentation disorders (post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation, melasma,
acanthosis nigricans) and keloid formation. It is less prone to skin cancer but presents
with more advanced disease when it occurs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Pigmentation} Determined by melanin produced by melanocytes in the stratum basale. Eumelanin
(brown-black) and pheomelanin (red-yellow) are the two types. Melanin production is
stimulated by UV radiation through melanocyte-stimulating hormone (MSH). Fitzpatrick
skin type classification (I to VI) describes pigmentation and sun response.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Tags} Benign fibroepithelial polyps occurring in intertriginous areas (neck, axillae, groin,
under breasts). They are associated with obesity, insulin resistance, and pregnancy.
Skin tags are asymptomatic but may become irritated or bleed. Treatment includes
snipping, cryotherapy, electrocautery, and shave excision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skull} Bony framework of the head with 22 bones: cranium (8: frontal, 2 parietal, 2 temporal,
occipital, sphenoid, ethmoid) and facial (14). Joined by sutures: coronal, sagittal,
lambdoid, squamous. The skull base has foramina for cranial nerves and vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skull fracture} Skull fracture examines skull fracture; interpretation depends on the specimen or body region, test method, reference range, and clinical question.
linear, depressed, basilar, or comminuted, with potential for intracranial injury and
cerebrospinal fluid leak.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SLAP Lesion} Superior labrum anterior to posterior tear. Associated with overhead activities, trauma.
Treatment: rest, physical therapy, surgical repair if symptomatic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SLAP Tears} Tears of the superior glenoid labrum extending from anterior to posterior, sometimes involving the
biceps tendon attachment. They may cause pain, clicking, or instability, especially with overhead activity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep} A naturally recurring physiological state characterized by altered consciousness,
reduced sensory responsiveness, and distinct brain wave patterns that is essential for
physical restoration, memory consolidation, and metabolic regulation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep (Geriatric)} Sleep patterns and sleep problems in older adults, including insomnia, sleep apnea, circadian changes,
and medication-related disturbances.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Geriatric

\medterm{Sleep Apnea} Repeated pauses or reductions in breathing during sleep. It may be obstructive, central, or mixed and can
disturb sleep and oxygenation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Apnea (Dental)} Oral appliance for obstructive sleep apnea. Mandibular advancement device. Indications:
mild to moderate OSA, CPAP intolerance.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Dental

\medterm{Sleep Apnea (Obstructive Sleep Apnea - OSA)} A sleep disorder characterized by repeated episodes of partial or complete upper airway
obstruction during sleep (obstructive sleep apnea—OSA, most common) or cessation of
respiratory drive (central sleep apnea—CSA), causing intermittent hypoxia, hypercapnia,
and sleep fragmentation.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Obstructive Sleep Apnea - OSA

\medterm{Sleep Bruxism} Repeated grinding or clenching of the teeth during sleep, which may cause tooth wear, jaw pain, or headaches.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Hygiene} A set of behavioral and environmental practices designed to promote consistent,
uninterrupted, and restorative sleep. Key elements include maintaining a regular sleep
schedule, creating a cool, dark, and quiet sleep environment, avoiding caffeine and
electronic screens before bedtime, and engaging in relaxing pre-sleep routines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Medications and Supplements} Pharmacological agents used to treat insomnia and other sleep disorders when behavioral
interventions alone are insufficient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Paralysis} Temporary inability to move or speak when falling asleep or waking, while awareness is partly or fully preserved.
Episodes usually last seconds to minutes and may include frightening hallucinations. Recurrent episodes
can be associated with sleep deprivation, irregular sleep, narcolepsy, or other sleep disorders.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Studies (Polysomnography)} Sleep tests, most commonly polysomnography, are diagnostic studies used to evaluate
sleep architecture and identify sleep disorders. Polysomnography records brain waves,
eye movements, heart rate, breathing patterns, blood oxygen levels, and limb movements
throughout the night in a controlled laboratory setting.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Polysomnography

\medterm{Sleep-Related Hypoventilation} Inadequate ventilation during sleep causing elevated carbon dioxide, oxygen
desaturation, or both. Causes include obesity, neuromuscular disease, chest-wall
disorders, chronic lung disease, medications, and central respiratory-control
abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleepwalking} Sleepwalking addresses care related to sleepwalking, including practical measures, warning signs, follow-up, and decisions to discuss with a clinician.
sleep, usually with little or no memory afterward. It is more common in children and may
be triggered by sleep deprivation, stress, fever, or other sleep disorders; injury
prevention and evaluation of recurrent or dangerous episodes are important.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleeve Gastrectomy} Removal ofmajor stomach, leaving sleeve. Restrictive procedure. Lower malabsorption risk
than bypass. Effective weight loss.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sliding Filament} The mechanism of muscle contraction in which actin filaments slide past myosin
filaments, shortening the sarcomere without changing filament lengths. Myosin
cross-bridges bind actin, undergo a power stroke using ATP hydrolysis, detach, and
reattach to new actin sites in a cyclic manner.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Slipped Capital Femoral Epiphysis} Displacement of the femoral head relative to the femoral neck through the growth plate
(physis) in adolescents. Most common hip disorder in adolescents (10-16 years). Risk
factors: obesity, male sex, African American, and endocrine disorders (hypothyroidism,
growth hormone deficiency).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Slit Lamp Biomicroscopy} A fundamental ophthalmological examination technique that uses a high-intensity,
adjustable slit beam of light combined with a binocular microscope to provide a
magnified, stereoscopic view of the anterior segment and, with the use of auxiliary
lenses, the posterior segment of the eye.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Airway Disease} Disease affecting airways with diameter < 2 mm (bronchioles). It is the earliest site of
damage in COPD and is often subclinical until advanced. It is characterized by
narrowing, mucus plugging, and inflammation of small airways. PFTs may show air trapping
(increased RV/TLC) before FEV1 decline.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Bowel Follow-Through} Extended upper GI series tracking barium through small bowel.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Bowel Obstruction} Partial or complete blockage of the small intestine. Common causes include adhesions,
hernias, tumors, and inflammatory narrowing. Symptoms may include abdominal pain,
vomiting, distension, and inability to pass stool or gas.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Bowel Series} X-ray imaging of small intestine using barium. Evaluates Crohn disease, tumors,
obstructions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Cell Carcinoma} A high-grade neuroendocrine carcinoma, most often of the lung, strongly associated with
smoking. Highly responsive initially to chemotherapy and radiation but prone to rapid
recurrence and early brain metastases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Cell Lung Cancer} Aggressive lung cancer, strongly associated with smoking. Early metastasis, responds to
chemotherapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Fiber Neuropathy} Damage to small sensory and autonomic nerve fibers, often causing burning pain, tingling, altered temperature or
pain sensation, abnormal sweating, or gastrointestinal and cardiovascular symptoms. Diabetes, autoimmune disease,
vitamin deficiency, toxins, and inherited disorders may contribute; evaluation seeks an underlying cause.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Intestinal Bacterial Overgrowth} An abnormal increase or change in bacteria within the small intestine, often causing bloating, abdominal
pain, nausea, diarrhea, fullness, weight loss, or nutrient deficiency. Causes include impaired intestinal movement,
structural problems, surgery, and some systemic diseases; persistent symptoms require medical evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small Intestine} Longest GI segment (approximately 6 meters) from pylorus to ileocecal valve. Primary
nutrient digestion and absorption site. Three segments: duodenum (25 cm,
chyme/bile/pancreatic enzymes), jejunum (2.5 m, prominent folds/villi for absorption),
ileum (3.5 m, Peyer's patches for immunity).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Small-bowel volvulus} Twisting of the small intestine around its mesentery, causing mechanical obstruction and
potentially compromising mesenteric blood flow. It may be primary (idiopathic, more
common in developing countries) or secondary to adhesions, malrotation, hernias, or
intraluminal tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smallpox} Smallpox was a severe contagious variola-virus disease with fever and a distinctive progressive pustular rash; global eradication was achieved through vaccination.
rash; it was eradicated through vaccination, although variola virus is retained in
authorized high-security laboratories.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SMART Goals for Antibiotic Stewardship} Stewardship objectives that are specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-limited. They define a
clear antimicrobial-use or patient-outcome target and a way to monitor progress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SMILE} Small incision lenticule extraction, commonly known as SMILE, is a minimally invasive
refractive surgical procedure that corrects myopia and myopic astigmatism by creating
and removing a thin, disc-shaped piece of corneal stroma called a lenticule through a
small incision, without creating a corneal flap.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smile Design} Planning smile based on facial features, gum display, tooth proportions. Digital smile
design software available.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smith-Lemli-Opitz Syndrome} An autosomal recessive disorder of cholesterol metabolism caused by mutations in DHCR7,
encoding 7-dehydrocholesterol reductase, the final enzyme in the cholesterol
biosynthetic pathway.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smith-Magenis Syndrome} A genetic neurodevelopmental disorder, usually involving deletion of 17p11.2 or an RAI1
variant. It causes developmental delay, intellectual disability, distinctive facial
features, sleep disturbance, behavioral dysregulation, and variable congenital
abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smoke Evacuator} A device that captures and filters airborne surgical plume generated by electrosurgery,
laser use, or ultrasonic energy. Evacuation reduces exposure to particulate matter,
toxic gases, and potentially infectious biologic material.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smoke Inhalation Injury} Pulmonary injury caused by inhalation of toxic products of combustion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smoking Cessation} The process of stopping tobacco or nicotine use. It may involve counseling, behavioral support, medicines, or nicotine
replacement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smooth Muscle} Involuntary, non-striated muscle found in the walls of hollow organs and blood vessels.
It produces slow, sustained contractions under autonomic, hormonal, and local control.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smothering} Mechanical obstruction of the external nose and mouth that prevents effective
ventilation. It may be caused by a covering, compression, or an obstructing soft
material; findings are often nonspecific and must be interpreted with the scene,
history, and complete examination.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Snapping Hip Syndrome} Hip pain/clicking from tendon snapping over bony prominence. Internal (iliopsoas),
external (IT band), intra-articular (labral tear). Treatment: physical therapy,
injection, surgery if refractory.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sneddon Syndrome} A non-inflammatory thrombotic vasculopathy characterized by livedo racemosa and
recurrent ischemic cerebrovascular events. Headache, hypertension, cognitive impairment,
and cardiac-valve abnormalities may occur; evaluation addresses vascular risk and
autoimmune or thrombophilic causes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Snoring and Sleep Apnea} Snoring is the sound produced by vibrating soft tissues in the upper airway during
sleep, often resulting from partial airway obstruction. Sleep apnea is a more serious
condition characterized by repeated episodes of complete or partial airway collapse
during sleep, leading to intermittent hypoxia, frequent arousal, and fragmented sleep.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SNRIs} Serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors are a class of antidepressant drugs that
inhibit the reuptake of both serotonin and norepinephrine from the synaptic cleft,
increasing both serotonergic and noradrenergic neurotransmission. Common SNRIs include
venlafaxine, duloxetine, desvenlafaxine, and levomilnacipran.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Social Anxiety Disorder} Marked fear or anxiety about one or more social situations in which the individual may
be scrutinized by others. The individual fears acting in a way or showing anxiety
symptoms that will be negatively evaluated.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Social Isolation} Limited contact or interaction with other people. Persistent isolation may adversely affect mental and
physical health.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sodium} Major extracellular cation. Fluid balance, nerve function, muscle contraction.
Recommended intake <2300 mg/day. Excess associated with hypertension. Sources: salt,
processed foods.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sodium Bicarbonate Therapy} Alkalinizing agent for metabolic acidosis in CKD. Target serum bicarbonate greater than
22 mEq/L. Slows CKD progression and improves muscle wasting. Dose titrated to serum
levels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sodium Polystyrene Sulfonate} Potassium-binding resin for hyperkalemia. Risk: colonic necrosis, especially with
sorbitol.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soft Markers for Aneuploidy} Sonographic findings in the second trimester that are common in normal fetuses but have
a statistically higher prevalence in aneuploid fetuses (especially trisomy 21).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soft Tissue Injury} Damage to muscles, tendons, ligaments, fascia, skin, or other non-bony tissues, often caused by
trauma or overuse.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soft Tissue Rheumatism} Pain conditions affecting muscles, tendons, ligaments, bursae. Examples: frozen
shoulder, lateral epicondylitis, rotator cuff tendinopathy. Treatment: rest,
physiotherapy, injections, surgery if refractory.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soft Tissue Sarcoma} A group of malignant tumors arising in muscle, fat, fibrous tissue, blood vessels, or
other connective tissues. Treatment depends on the subtype, location, stage, and relationship to nearby structures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soft Tissue Tumors} Lipoma: most common benign soft tissue tumor, subcutaneous, encapsulated. Liposarcoma:
most common malignant STS in adults, deep, subtypes
(well-differentiated/dedifferentiated, myxoid/round cell, pleomorphic). Synovial
sarcoma: t(X;18), SS18-SSX, young adults, deep extremity, "triple sign" on MRI.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soft-tissue calcinosis} The deposition of insoluble calcium phosphate salts in soft tissues such as skin,
subcutaneous tissue, muscles, and tendons, which may be metastatic (due to
hypercalcaemia or hyperphosphataemia), dystrophic (in damaged or devitalised tissue), or
idiopathic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soil Contamination} Environmental toxicology. Heavy metals, pesticides, industrial waste. Remediation:
excavation, bioremediation, containment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Solar Urticaria} A rare form of inducible urticaria triggered by ultraviolet or visible light, producing
pruritic erythema and wheals within minutes on exposed skin. Symptoms usually improve
after removal from the light source. Diagnosis may involve phototesting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soleus Muscle} Broad flat deep posterior leg muscle from posterior tibia, fibula, and tendinous arch to
the calcaneus via the Achilles tendon. Innervated by tibial nerve (S1-S2). Powerful
ankle plantarflexor, the primary anti-gravity muscle for upright posture. One-joint
muscle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Solitary Pulmonary Nodule} Single, well-circumscribed lesion < 3 cm surrounded by normal lung. Differential
includes granuloma, hamartoma, and carcinoma. PET-CT and tissue sampling help determine
etiology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Solution} A homogeneous mixture in which a drug (solute) is dissolved in a liquid vehicle
(solvent, usually water, saline, alcohol, or polyethylene glycol). Solutions provide
rapid absorption (oral solutions, parenteral IV solutions), ease of swallowing, and
flexible dosing; they require preservatives and have limited stability.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Solvent Exposure} Contact with organic solvents by inhalation, ingestion, or skin absorption. Acute exposure may cause
irritation or central nervous system effects; repeated exposure may damage organs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatic Mutation} Acquired genetic change in cancer cells. Not inherited.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatic Symptom Disorder} Characterized by one or more somatic symptoms that are distressing or result in
significant disruption of daily life, accompanied by excessive thoughts, feelings, or
behaviors related to the somatic symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatic Variant} A DNA variant acquired after conception in a non-germline cell lineage. It is not
usually transmitted to offspring and may produce mosaic disease or contribute to cancer
when it affects growth-control pathways.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatostatin Analog} A class of synthetic peptides that mimic the actions of native somatostatin, a hormone
that inhibits the secretion of growth hormone, thyroid-stimulating hormone, insulin,
glucagon, gastrin, and other GI hormones.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatostatinoma} Rare pancreatic or duodenal tumor producing somatostatin causing diabetes, gallstones,
steatorrhea, and hypochlorhydria from suppression of insulin, gallbladder contraction,
pancreatic enzymes, and gastric acid respectively. Diagnosed by elevated somatostatin
levels. Treatment is surgical resection for localized disease.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatotropic pituitary adenoma} A benign neoplasm of the anterior pituitary arising from growth hormone-secreting cells,
causing acromegaly in adults and gigantism in children if occurring before epiphyseal
closure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sonographer} A healthcare professional trained to perform ultrasound scans (sonography).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sore Throat} Pain, scratchiness, or irritation of the throat, most often caused by pharyngitis,
typically viral but also bacterial, allergic, irritant, reflux, voice strain, traumatic,
or neoplastic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Source Control} A procedure or intervention that eliminates, drains, debrides, or otherwise controls an
anatomic focus of infection or contamination. Examples include drainage of an abscess,
removal of infected tissue or device, and repair of a perforation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Soy Protein Intolerance} Adverse gastrointestinal reaction to soy proteins, most often seen in infants and young
children. It may cause vomiting, diarrhoea, abdominal pain, blood or mucus in the stool,
feeding difficulty, or poor growth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spasm} A sudden, involuntary, sustained or repetitive contraction of a muscle or group of
muscles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spasticity} Spasticity concerns spasticity; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
reflex after an upper motor-neuron lesion. It may impair movement, hygiene, positioning,
comfort, gait, and function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spasticity Management} Assessment and treatment of abnormal muscle stiffness and spasms. Measures may include
stretching, therapy, positioning, medicines, injections, or surgery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spatial Resolution} Ability to distinguish small objects. PET: 4-6 mm. SPECT: 8-12 mm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Special Care Baby Unit} A ward within a hospital that provides specialist care for newborn babies who need
additional support but not intensive care.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Special Stain} A histochemical stain that highlights a particular tissue component, organism, or
deposited substance not adequately demonstrated by routine hematoxylin and eosin.
Examples include periodic acid-Schiff, Ziehl-Neelsen, Congo red, and Gomori methenamine
silver stains.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specific Absorbed Fraction} Fraction of emitted energy absorbed in target organ.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specific Activity} Radioactivity per unit mass of compound. High specific activity essential for receptor
targeting (low receptor density).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specific Gravity} Ratio of urine density to water density reflecting solute concentration. Normal 1.005 to
1.030. Less precise than osmolality but faster to measure. Elevated in dehydration,
glycosuria, proteinuria, and contrast. Decreased in diabetes insipidus and fluid
overload.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specific Learning Disorder} A neurodevelopmental disorder causing persistent difficulty acquiring or using academic skills in reading, writing, or
mathematics. The difficulties interfere with functioning and are not better explained by
another condition or inadequate instruction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specific Phobia} A disorder involving marked, persistent fear or anxiety about a particular object or situation.
The fear is excessive relative to the actual danger, leads to avoidance or intense distress,
and interferes with daily functioning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specificity} The proportion of people who do not have a disease who obtain a negative test result. A
highly specific test has few false-positive results and a positive result can provide
strong evidence for disease when applied to the intended population.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specimen Adequacy} The degree to which a collected clinical specimen is sufficient in quantity, correctly
identified, properly transported, and representative of the site being tested.
Inadequacy can cause invalid, misleading, or falsely negative results.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specimen Container} Container for collecting specimens. Types: urine cup, stool container, sputum cup,
biohazard bag.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specimen Cup} Container for urine or stool samples.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Specimen Transport Medium} A formulated medium that preserves the viability and diagnostic characteristics of
microorganisms during transport from the collection site to the laboratory. The required
medium, temperature, container, and transport time depend on the specimen and suspected
pathogen.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SPECT Scan (Single Photon Emission CT)} Nuclear medicine imaging using gamma-emitting radioisotopes (Tc-99m, Tl-201, I-123) with
a rotating gamma camera to create 3D images.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Single Photon Emission CT

\medterm{Spectral Analysis} Energy discrimination to reject scatter. Energy window typically 20\% centered on
photopeak.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Speculum} Device for opening body passage for examination. Types: vaginal, nasal, ear, anal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Speech} Phonation and articulation. Teeth, tongue, lips, palate involved. Dental prostheses may
affect speech initially.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Speech Delay} A delay in language development compared to peers: by 12 months—no single words; 18
months—<10-20 words/no word combinations by 24 months; or regression.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Speech Discrimination} Speech discrimination testing, also known as word recognition scoring, measures the
ability to understand speech at a comfortable listening level, and it is an important
component of the audiometric evaluation that provides information about cochlear and
neural function beyond what pure tone audiometry alone can reveal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
word recognition scoring, measures the.

\medterm{Speech Therapy} Assessment and treatment of disorders affecting speech, language, voice, communication, or swallowing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Speech-Language Pathology Assessment} Evaluate communication, swallowing, cognition. Standardized tests, clinical observation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sperm} The male gamete (reproductive cell), produced in the seminiferous tubules of the testis
through spermatogenesis. Sperm consists of: head (contains the haploid nucleus and
acrosome containing enzymes for egg penetration), midpiece (contains mitochondria for
energy), and tail (flagellum for motility).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spermatocele} Benign, painless, fluid-filled cystic retention cavity arising from the epididymis
(usually the head/caput), containing avascular fluid and non-viable spermatozoa.
Etiology: obstruction of an efferent ductule of the epididymis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spermatogonium} A germ cell in the testis that divides and develops into primary spermatocytes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spermarche} The onset of sperm production and the first ejaculation, marking an important stage of male puberty.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spermatorrhea} Involuntary discharge of semen, especially without orgasm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sphenoid Bone} Butterfly-shaped bone at the skull base, with body (sphenoid sinuses), greater/lesser
wings, and pterygoid processes. The sella turcica houses the pituitary gland.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sphenoid Sinusitis} Inflammation or infection of the sphenoid sinus, which lies deep behind the nasal cavity. It may cause
deep headache, facial pain, or visual symptoms and can rarely spread to nearby nerves or the brain;
persistent or severe symptoms require medical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sphenoidal Sinus} A paired air-filled sinus within the sphenoid bone at the base of the skull. It lies near important nerves and blood
vessels, so inflammation or surgery in this area requires careful assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spherocytes} Small, dark, sphere-shaped red blood cells lacking central pallor. Formed when RBC
membrane is lost without loss of volume, reducing the surface area-to-volume ratio.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sphygmomanometer} An instrument used to measure arterial blood pressure, consisting of an inflatable cuff and a
pressure-measuring system. Manual devices use Korotkoff sounds; automated devices use an
electronic sensor. Correct cuff size and technique are essential for reliable measurement.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spider angioma} Spider angioma concerns spider angioma; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
resembling a spider, most commonly found on the face, neck, and upper trunk. They are
associated with hyperestrogenic states including pregnancy, oral contraceptive use, and
chronic liver disease, particularly cirrhosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spider bite} A cutaneous envenomation resulting from the bite of a spider, ranging from minor local
reactions to severe systemic envenomation. The brown recluse spider may cause
loxoscelism with necrotic skin lesions, while the black widow spider may cause
latrodectism with muscle cramps, hypertension, and autonomic dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spina Bifida} A neural tube defect: incomplete closure of the neural tube (days 22-28), causing spinal
cord/meningeal malformation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spina Bifida Meningocele} A form of spina bifida where the meninges (but not neural tissue) herniate through a
vertebral defect, forming a CSF-filled sac. The spinal cord remains in its normal
position and is not involved. Presents as a visible, fluid-filled, translucent sac on
the back, typically without neurologic deficits.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spina Bifida Myelomeningocele} The most severe form of spina bifida, where both the meninges and neural tissue (spinal
cord and nerve roots) herniate through a vertebral defect. Presents as a visible, flat
or sac-like lesion on the back with neurologic deficits below the level of the lesion
(paralysis, sensory loss, bowel/bladder dysfunction).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spina Bifida Occulta} A usually mild form of spina bifida in which the vertebral arch does not fully close
without herniation of the spinal cord or meninges. It is often asymptomatic but may be associated with a skin dimple,
hair tuft, or other finding over the lower spine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Analgesia} Intrathecal administration of a local anesthetic and/or opioid primarily to provide
analgesia, often with limited motor block. Intrathecal opioids can extend postoperative
pain relief but may cause pruritus, urinary retention, respiratory depression, and other
opioid effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Anesthesia} Injection of local anesthetic into the lumbar subarachnoid space to produce temporary
sensory, motor, and sympathetic blockade below the level of injection. Complications
include hypotension, bradycardia, urinary retention, post-dural puncture headache, high
or total spinal block, infection, and bleeding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Anesthesia for Cesarean Delivery} A neuraxial anesthetic technique used for elective and many emergency cesarean
deliveries. A small dose of local anesthetic (hyperbaric bupivacaine 12-15 mg) with
opioid (fentanyl 15-25 mcg, morphine 0.15-0.25 mg) is injected into the subarachnoid
space. Produces rapid surgical anesthesia (T4 sensory level for cesarean).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Angiography} Contrast imaging of spinal vessels. Evaluates vascular malformations.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Cord} The cylindrical bundle of nerve fibers extending from the medulla oblongata (at the
foramen magnum) to the conus medullaris (L1--L2 in adults), surrounded by the vertebral
column and meninges (dura mater, arachnoid mater, pia mater). The spinal cord is divided
into 31 segments: 8 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 sacral, and 1 coccygeal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Cord Compression} Oncologic emergency. Back pain, weakness, sensory loss, bowel/bladder dysfunction. MRI
diagnostic. Treatment: steroids, radiation, surgery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Cord Injury} Spinal cord injury: ASIA impairment scale (A-E). Complete (ASIA A) vs incomplete.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal dysraphism} A group of congenital malformations resulting from incomplete closure of the neural tube
during embryonic development, affecting the spinal cord and overlying vertebral
structures. Includes open defects (myelomeningocele) and closed defects (spina bifida
occulta, lipomyelomeningocele, dermal sinus).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Epidural Abscess} A collection of pus in the spinal epidural space, usually caused by haematogenous or
contiguous infection. It may present with severe local back pain, fever, spinal
tenderness, radicular pain, neurological deficit, or sphincter disturbance, although the
classic triad is often incomplete.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Fusion} Surgical union of two or more vertebrae using bone graft or bone graft substitutes with
instrumentation (rods, screws, cages). Approaches: posterior (posterolateral fusion,
transforaminal lumbar interbody fusion TLIF), anterior (anterior lumbar interbody fusion
ALIF), and lateral (extreme lateral interbody fusion XLIF).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Muscular Atrophy} An inherited disorder causing progressive loss of motor neurons and muscle weakness, often beginning in infancy
or childhood but sometimes appearing in adulthood. Severity varies; breathing, swallowing, and mobility
may be affected. Early diagnosis allows genetic counseling and disease-modifying care.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Nerve} A mixed nerve formed by the union of dorsal and ventral roots as it exits the vertebral
canal. There are 31 pairs, carrying sensory, motor, and autonomic fibers to and from the
spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Precautions} Immobilization of spine in trauma patients to prevent neurological injury. Indications:
mechanism (fall >3 feet, MVC, gunshot), altered mental status, focal neurological
deficit, midline spinal tenderness. Technique: cervical collar, backboard, log roll.
Clearance: NEXUS criteria or Canadian C-spine rule.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Shock} A state of transient physiological areflexia and flaccid paralysis occurring immediately
after acute spinal cord injury, below the level of the lesion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Stenosis} Narrowing of the spinal canal causing compression of the spinal cord or nerve roots.
Lumbar stenosis (most common): neurogenic claudication (leg pain/weakness with walking,
relieved by sitting/leaning forward), often bilateral.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spindle-cell sarcoma} A group of sarcomas characterized by elongated, spindle-shaped malignant cells. It
includes entities like leiomyosarcoma, fibrosarcoma, and malignant peripheral nerve
sheath tumor, with variable behavior.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spine} The vertebral column, consisting of stacked vertebrae that support the body and protect the spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spine Disorders} The spine, or vertebral column, is a complex bony and soft-tissue structure that
provides axial support, protects the spinal cord, and enables flexible movement of the
trunk. It consists of 33 vertebrae divided into cervical, thoracic, lumbar, sacral, and
coccygeal regions, separated by intervertebral discs that act as shock absorbers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinocerebellar Ataxias} A group of autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorders characterized by progressive
cerebellar ataxia, often with additional neurological features. Over forty different SCA
types have been identified, with SCA1, SCA2, SCA3 (Machado-Joseph disease, the most
common worldwide), SCA6, and SCA7 being the most prevalent.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spirochaete} A slender, spiral-shaped bacterium of the group Spirochaetes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spirochete} A thin, coiled gram-negative bacterium with internal periplasmic flagella conferring
corkscrew motility, requiring darkfield or special stains. Pathogenic genera include
Treponema (syphilis), Borrelia (Lyme disease, relapsing fever), and Leptospira
(leptospirosis), diagnosed mainly by serology and darkfield microscopy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spirometry} Most common PFT measuring air volume and flow during forced breathing. Key measurements:
FVC, FEV1, FEV1/FVC ratio. Used to diagnose and classify obstructive and restrictive
diseases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spironolactone} A nonselective aldosterone (mineralocorticoid) receptor antagonist and potassium-sparing
diuretic. Used for heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (NYHA II-IV—reduces
mortality, often with ACE/ARB + beta-blocker), resistant hypertension, primary
hyperaldosteronism, ascites in cirrhosis, and acne/hirsutism in women (off-label).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spleen} Largest lymphoid organ in the left upper quadrant under ribs 9-11, approximately 12 cm
long and 150-200 g. Red pulp filters blood (removing old/damaged RBCs and platelets);
white pulp mounts immune responses (lymphoid follicles and periarteriolar lymphoid
sheaths). Enclosed by a fibroelastic capsule with trabeculae.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spleen Hilum} Concave medial surface where the tortuous splenic artery (celiac trunk branch) enters
and splenic vein exits, with lymphatics and nerves. Artery divides into 5 segmental
branches at the hilum (basis for partial splenectomy). Splenic vein joins the superior
mesenteric vein forming the portal vein.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splenectomy} Indications: trauma (grade IV/V, hemodynamic instability), ITP (refractory), hereditary
spherocytosis/elliptocytosis, thalassemia major, sickle cell (sequestration crisis), TTP
(refractory), lymphoma staging (historical), hypersplenism, splenic cysts/tumors,
Wandering spleen. Laparoscopic preferred.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splenic cyst} A fluid-filled lesion within the spleen, classified as true cysts with an epithelial
lining, including congenital, epidermoid, and dermoid cysts, or pseudocysts without a
lining, typically resulting from trauma, infection, or pancreatitis. Most are
asymptomatic and discovered incidentally on imaging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splenic Infarction} Splenic infarction occurs when the splenic artery or its branches are occluded,
typically by emboli from the heart. The spleen has end-arterial supply with limited
collateral circulation, making it vulnerable to infarction even with small vessel
occlusion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splenic Rupture} Traumatic or spontaneous splenic injury. CT diagnosis. May require splenectomy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splenomegaly} Splenomegaly is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
Splenomegaly can be detected by percussion (Castell sign—dullness in the lowest
intercostal space on inspiration) and palpation (normal spleen is not palpable; a
palpable spleen indicates at least 2--3 times enlargement).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splice-Site Variant} A variant affecting the sequences required for accurate removal of introns and joining
of exons during RNA processing. It may cause abnormal transcripts, exon skipping, intron
retention, or reduced functional protein.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splint} Immobilizes injured body part. Types: aluminum, plastic, canvas, pneumatic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splint (Dental)} Fixation of mobile teeth or fractured tooth. Wire and composite, orthodontic bracket
splint.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Dental

\medterm{Splinter Hemorrhages} Linear hemorrhages under the nail bed, oriented longitudinally. In infective
endocarditis, they are caused by septic microemboli to the nail bed capillaries. They
are a minor Duke criterion. Splinter hemorrhages can also result from trauma, psoriasis,
and systemic vasculitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splitting} A psychological defense pattern in which a person or situation is viewed as entirely good or entirely bad, with difficulty integrating
positive and negative qualities. It can occur in several mental-health conditions and is
not diagnostic by itself.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spondyloarthritis} Group of inflammatory arthritis associated with HLA-B27. Includes ankylosing
spondylitis, psoriatic arthritis, reactive arthritis, enteropathic arthritis. Features:
axial involvement, enthesitis, dactylitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spondylolisthesis} Spondylolisthesis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
II, most common in children/adolescents): defect in pars interarticularis
(spondylolysis), often L5-S1. Degenerative (Type III, most common in adults): facet
joint and disc degeneration, often L4-L5.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sponge (Vascular)} A sterile absorbent material used during vascular procedures to control minor bleeding or protect
tissue.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis} Infection of ascitic fluid without an identifiable intra-abdominal source representing a
serious complication of cirrhosis with ascites. The pathogenesis involves impaired
immune function, bacterial translocation from the intestinal lumen, and seeding of
ascitic fluid which provides a favorable medium for bacterial growth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spontaneous Breathing Trial} A period of unsupported breathing to assess a patient's readiness for extubation. The
patient breathes through a T-piece or with minimal pressure support for 30--120 minutes.
Successful SBT predicts successful extubation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spontaneous Pneumothorax} Pneumothorax without trauma. Primary in healthy individuals; secondary with underlying
lung disease. Treatment depends on size.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spore} A resistant reproductive or survival structure produced by some fungi and bacteria. Bacterial endospores can
survive unfavorable environmental conditions and germinate when conditions become
suitable. Their resistance has important implications for infection control and
sterilization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sporotrichosis} Sporotrichosis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
in soil, moss, and plant matter (rose thorns, sphagnum moss).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sports Rehabilitation} Rehabilitation that restores safe movement and performance after a sports-related injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sprain} An injury to a ligament (connective tissue connecting bone to bone) caused by excessive
stretching or tearing. Grading: Grade I (mild stretching, microscopic tears), Grade II
(partial tear with laxity), Grade III (complete tear with instability).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sprengel Deformity} A congenital elevation of the scapula due to failure of the scapula to descend from its
embryonic cervical position to its normal thoracic position during weeks 9-12 of
gestation. The scapula is higher, smaller, and rotated inferiorly compared to the normal
side. Range of motion is limited, especially in shoulder abduction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spur-cell hemolytic anemia} A severe hemolytic anemia seen in advanced liver disease where acanthocytosis develops
due to altered membrane cholesterol, causing premature red cell destruction in the
spleen.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sputum} Mucus or phlegm produced in the lower airways and expelled by coughing. Its amount, color, odor, or blood content
may provide clues to infection, inflammation, or bleeding, but appearance alone does not establish
a diagnosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Squamous Cell Carcinoma} Cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma is a malignant keratinocyte tumor commonly arising on
chronically sun-exposed skin or in areas of chronic inflammation or immunosuppression.
It may present as a scaly, indurated, hyperkeratotic papule, plaque, or ulcer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Squint} A misalignment of the visual axes of the two eyes, where one eye is deviated inward
(esotropia), outward (exotropia), upward (hypertropia), or downward (hypotropia)
relative to the other, disrupting binocular vision and potentially leading to amblyopia
in children. See Strabismus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SSRIs} Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors are a class of antidepressant drugs that
selectively inhibit the reuptake of serotonin from the synaptic cleft into the
presynaptic neuron by blocking the serotonin transporter, increasing serotonergic
neurotransmission.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{ST Segment} The isoelectric period between ventricular depolarization (QRS) and repolarization (T
wave). It corresponds to the plateau phase of ventricular action potentials. ST segment
elevation (>1 mm in limb leads or >2 mm in precordial leads) suggests acute myocardial
infarction, pericarditis, or early repolarization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{ST-Segment Elevation} Elevation of the ECG ST segment above the isoelectric baseline, reflecting abnormal
ventricular repolarization. It may indicate acute coronary occlusion but can also occur
in pericarditis, early repolarization, ventricular aneurysm, or other conditions;
interpretation requires clinical context and serial testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{St. John's Wort} Herbal supplement. CYP3A4 inducer, reduces efficacy of many drugs (oral contraceptives,
warfarin, antiretrovirals).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stab Wound} A penetrating sharp-force injury in which wound track depth exceeds skin defect length,
produced by a pointed or sharp-edged implement. The skin defect approximates blade width
only when perpendicular to Langer's lines, and a single-edged blade produces one sharp
and one blunt end.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stable Disease} Cancer neither growing nor shrinking significantly.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stage I} A cancer stage that generally indicates limited disease, although the exact definition and prognosis depend on the cancer type and staging system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stage II} A cancer stage whose meaning depends on the cancer type and may indicate a larger primary tumor or limited local spread without distant metastasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stage III} A cancer stage that often indicates more extensive local or regional disease, with the precise criteria depending on the cancer type.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stage IV} A cancer stage that generally indicates spread to distant organs or sites. Prognosis and treatment vary by cancer type and individual factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stages of Labor} Four stages describing the progression from uterine contractions to delivery of the
placenta. First stage: onset of regular contractions to full cervical dilatation (10
cm). Second stage: full dilatation to delivery of the baby. Third stage: delivery of the
baby to delivery of the placenta.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staghorn Calculus} A large renal stone that occupies the renal pelvis and extends into at least two
calyces, creating a cast of the collecting system. Composition: struvite (magnesium
ammonium phosphate), associated with urease-producing bacteria (Proteus mirabilis,
Klebsiella, Pseudomonas) causing urinary tract infection.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staining} A laboratory technique applying dyes to microbial smears or tissue sections to visualise
microorganisms and reveal structural characteristics. Major methods include Gram,
acid-fast, Giemsa, and fluorescent stains, providing rapid presumptive identification
from clinical specimens and guiding initial empiric therapy.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stainless Steel} Alloy for preformed crowns, orthodontic bands, instruments. Corrosion-resistant.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Standard of Care} The conduct, skill, diligence, and judgment a reasonably prudent practitioner of the
same specialty would exercise under similar circumstances; the benchmark for medical
negligence. It is established through guidelines, peer practice, and expert testimony
rather than fixed statute.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Standard Precautions} Precautions for all patient care. Examples: hand hygiene, PPE, safe injection,
respiratory hygiene.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Standardized Uptake Value} Quantitative PET measure: tissue activity divided by injected dose per body weight.
Normalizes for body size, injection dose. SUVmax: maximum pixel value in lesion.
SUVmean: average lesion uptake. Background: blood pool, liver.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stapedectomy} A microsurgical procedure performed to treat otosclerosis, a condition characterized by
abnormal bone remodeling in the otic capsule that fixes the stapes footplate in the oval
window, preventing its normal vibratory movement and causing progressive conductive
hearing loss.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stapes} The stapes, commonly known as the stirrup, is the smallest bone in the human body and
the most medially positioned ossicle in the middle ear, articulating with the incus at
the incudostapedial joint and transmitting sound vibrations to the inner ear through its
footplate, which is attached to the oval window by the annular ligament.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staph Infection} Infections caused by Staphylococcus aureus (and coagulase-negative staphylococci), a
gram-positive coccus in clusters.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staphylococcal Infection} An infection caused by Staphylococcus aureus or another Staphylococcus species. It may affect
the skin, bone, joints, lungs, heart valves, blood, or other tissues. Diagnosis and
antimicrobial treatment depend on the site, severity, and susceptibility of the organism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staphylococcal Pneumonia} Severe pneumonia from S. aureus, often post-influenza. May cause necrotizing pneumonia,
lung abscess, and empyema.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staphylococcal scalded skin} A toxin-mediated Staphylococcus aureus infection causing superficial separation and tenderness of
the outer skin, usually in infants or young children. Widespread redness and fragile
blisters may rupture to leave painful, moist areas resembling a scald burn and require
prompt medical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staphylococcus aureus} A gram-positive, catalase-positive, coagulase-positive coccus that causes a wide range
of infections including skin infections, pneumonia, endocarditis, and osteomyelitis. S.
aureus produces numerous virulence factors including toxins, enzymes, and surface
proteins. MRSA strains carry the mecA gene encoding PBP2a.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staple (Vascular)} A small metal or polymer device used to close tissue or connect vascular structures.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Stargardt Disease} The most common inherited macular dystrophy, usually autosomal recessive and caused by
ABCA4 gene mutations. Presents in childhood or adolescence with progressive central
vision loss, retinal flecks, and bull's-eye maculopathy; no curative treatment exists.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Starling Forces} Starling forces describe the balance of hydrostatic and oncotic pressures that govern
fluid movement across capillary walls, determining the distribution of fluid between the
intravascular and extravascular compartments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Statin} An inhibitor of HMG-CoA reductase that lowers hepatic cholesterol synthesis and
increases hepatic LDL-receptor expression. Statins reduce atherosclerotic cardiovascular
events; adverse effects include muscle symptoms, rare severe myopathy, and liver-enzyme
abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Statins in Diabetes} Use of HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors to reduce atherosclerotic cardiovascular risk in
people with diabetes. The decision, intensity, and treatment target are individualized
according to age, cardiovascular risk, kidney function, and other clinical factors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Station} The relationship of the fetal presenting part to the level of the ischial spines,
measured in centimeters.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Status Asthmaticus} A severe, life-threatening asthma exacerbation unresponsive to standard bronchodilator
therapy. Characterized by severe airflow obstruction, hypoxemia, and hypercapnia.
Treatment includes continuous nebulized albuterol, IV magnesium sulfate, systemic
corticosteroids, and possibly intubation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Status Epilepticus} A seizure lasting at least 5 minutes or recurrent seizures without recovery to baseline
consciousness between them, requiring urgent treatment. Ongoing convulsive or
nonconvulsive status can cause neuronal injury, systemic complications, and death.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Steady State} The condition where the rate of drug administration equals the rate of elimination,
resulting in constant average plasma concentrations with repeated dosing. During regular
dosing at fixed intervals, steady state is achieved after approximately 4 to 5
half-lives, regardless of the dosing frequency or dose size.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Steal Syndrome} Ischemia of distal extremity due to blood flow diversion to bypass graft or AV fistula.
Most common: upper extremity after AV fistula creation. Symptoms: hand pain, pallor,
numbness, weakness. Treatment: banding, ligation, revision.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Steatorrhea} The passage of excess fat in the stool, causing greasy, pale, foul-smelling, floating
stools—a hallmark of fat malabsorption.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stem Cell Transplantation} Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) replaces diseased or ablated bone marrow
with healthy donor or autologous stem cells.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{STEMI} ST-elevation myocardial infarction: transmural myocardial necrosis from acute total
occlusion of a coronary artery, an emergency requiring immediate reperfusion. ECG:
ST-segment elevation in contiguous leads (or new LBBB); troponin elevated.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stenosis} Narrowing of vessel lumen. Causes: atherosclerosis, thrombosis, dissection, external
compression. Symptoms depend on location and severity. Treatment: medical, angioplasty,
stenting, bypass.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stenosis of the left anterior descending artery} Narrowing of the left anterior descending coronary artery, which can
reduce blood flow to the front of the heart and cause ischemia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stenotrophomonas maltophilia resistance} Resistance in Stenotrophomonas maltophilia results from intrinsic and acquired
mechanisms that limit the activity of several antibiotics. Treatment should be guided by
the site of infection, susceptibility testing, illness severity, and specialist advice.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stent} Stent concerns stent; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
cobalt-chrome, nitinol), polymer. Types: bare-metal, drug-eluting. Applications:
coronary, peripheral, biliary, ureteral, tracheal. Complications: restenosis,
thrombosis, migration, fracture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stent (Vascular)} A small mesh or tube placed inside a narrowed or blocked vessel to help keep it open.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Stent Balloon (Vascular)} A balloon catheter used to expand a stent against the wall of a blood vessel.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Stent Graft} A fabric-covered stent placed inside a blood vessel to reinforce the vessel wall or exclude an aneurysm from blood flow.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stent Placement} Image-guided deployment of stent. Vascular, biliary, ureteral, esophageal. Maintains
patency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stent-Graft (Vascular)} A fabric-covered vascular stent used to reinforce a vessel or exclude an aneurysm or leak.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Stenting} Placement of mesh tube to keep vessel open. Drug-eluting stents reduce restenosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stepping Reflex} A primitive reflex present from birth to 2 months, elicited by holding the infant
upright with feet touching a flat surface. The infant makes stepping-like movements as
if walking. Disappears by 2 months as voluntary motor control develops. Reappears around
9-10 months as voluntary walking emerges.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stereotactic Biopsy} Biopsy using stereotactic guidance. Breast, brain, lung.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stereotactic Body Radiation Therapy} A highly precise form of external radiation delivering high-dose ablative treatment in
few fractions, used for early-stage lung cancer and selected liver, pancreatic, spinal,
and kidney tumors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stereotactic Radiosurgery} Single high-dose radiation to small target. Used for brain metastases, AVMs, acoustic
neuroma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sterile Field} A designated area maintained free of viable microorganisms during an invasive procedure.
Only sterile equipment and materials may contact the field or the sterile portions of
instruments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sterile Site} A body site or specimen type expected to contain no viable microorganisms in health,
such as blood, cerebrospinal fluid, synovial fluid, or tissue from a closed space.
Recovery of a pathogen from a sterile site is generally clinically significant, subject
to contamination assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sterilisation} A permanent method of contraception. Female sterilization (tubal occlusion): the
fallopian tubes are blocked or cut to prevent the egg from reaching the uterus,
performed by laparoscopy (clip or diathermy) or at the time of cesarean delivery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sterilization} A process eliminating all microbial life including spores, achieving a sterility
assurance level of at least $10^{-6}$.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sternoclavicular Joint} A saddle-type synovial joint between the clavicle and manubrium of the sternum. It is
the only bony connection between the upper limb and axial skeleton and permits movement
of the shoulder girdle.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sternocleidomastoid Muscle} Strap-like anterior neck muscle from manubrium and medial clavicle to the mastoid
process. Innervated by CN XI and C2-C3. Divides neck into anterior/posterior triangles.
Unilaterally: laterally flexes to opposite side, rotates face to same side. Bilaterally:
flexes neck, assists forced inspiration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sternum} Flat bone in the anterior midline thorax, with manubrium, body, and xiphoid process. The
sternal angle (of Louis) at the manubriosternal junction is a key landmark at the 2nd
costal cartilage, T4-T5, and tracheal bifurcation. Common site for bone marrow biopsy.
Median sternotomy is the standard cardiac surgical approach.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stethoscope} Acoustic device for listening to internal body sounds. Components: chest piece (bell and
diaphragm), tubing, earpieces. Bell: low-frequency sounds. Diaphragm: high-frequency
sounds. Essential for cardiac, pulmonary, abdominal auscultation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stevens-Johnson Syndrome} A severe, life-threatening mucocutaneous reaction characterized by widespread skin
erythema, necrosis, and bullae formation with mucosal involvement (oral, ocular,
genital). It is most commonly caused by drug reactions (sulfonamides, anticonvulsants,
allopurinol, NSAIDs) and infections (Mycoplasma pneumoniae).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stickler Syndrome} An autosomal dominant connective tissue disorder caused by mutations in collagen genes
(COL2A1 most common, also COL11A1, COL11A2, COL9A1).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stillbirth} Intrauterine fetal death (IUFD) at or after 20-24 weeks (definition varies by country);
fetal death before viability (<20 weeks) is miscarriage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stimulant Use Disorder} A problematic pattern of stimulant use (cocaine, amphetamines, methamphetamine, MDMA)
leading to significant impairment. Stimulants increase dopamine and norepinephrine
activity. Withdrawal is characterized by fatigue, depression, increased appetite,
hypersomnia, psychomotor retardation, and vivid unpleasant dreams.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stippling (Tattooing)} Patterned dermal abrasions and punctate hemorrhages surrounding a firearm entrance
wound, caused by unburned and partially burned gunpowder grains striking the skin at
close range (15--75 cm).



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Tattooing

\medterm{Stochastic Effects} Random radiation effects with no threshold. Cancer, genetic effects. Probability
increases with dose.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stoma Complications} Complications related to intestinal or urinary stomas.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stomach} J-shaped muscular organ in the left upper quadrant, connecting esophagus to duodenum.
Regions: cardia, fundus, body, antrum, pylorus. Functions: food storage, mechanical
churning, chemical digestion (pepsin, HCl), intrinsic factor secretion (B12 absorption).
Mucosa has rugae (folds) allowing expansion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stomach Cancer} Malignant cancer arising in the stomach, most often an adenocarcinoma. Risk factors include Helicobacter
pylori infection, smoking, certain dietary exposures, chronic gastric inflammation, and
inherited syndromes. Symptoms, stage, and tumor type guide treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stomach Ulcer} An open sore in the lining of the stomach, commonly associated with Helicobacter pylori infection or medicines such as NSAIDs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stomatitis} Inflammation of the oral mucosa, presenting as erythema, ulceration, pain, and
difficulty eating.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{STOPP/START} Screening Tool of Older Person's Prescriptions (STOPP) and Potential Older Person's
Prescriptions (START). Identifies inappropriate prescribing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Storage Diseases} A group of inherited metabolic disorders characterized by accumulation of substrates
within cells due to specific enzyme deficiencies. Lysosomal storage diseases result from
defects in lysosomal hydrolytic enzymes, leading to accumulation of undigested
macromolecules within lysosomes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strabismus} Strabismus concerns strabismus; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
(esotropia), outward (exotropia), upward (hypertropia), or downward (hypotropia)
relative to the other, disrupting binocular vision and potentially leading to amblyopia
in children.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strain} An injury to a muscle or tendon (connective tissue connecting muscle to bone) caused by
excessive stretching, contraction, or overload. Grading: Grade I (mild stretching with
few muscle fibers torn), Grade II (moderate partial tear), Grade III (complete
muscle/tendon rupture).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strain Imaging} Echocardiographic or magnetic-resonance imaging that measures deformation of the myocardium
during contraction and relaxation. It can detect abnormalities in myocardial function that may not be apparent from
ejection fraction alone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strangulation} Compression of the neck that impairs breathing or blood flow to and from the brain. It may cause voice changes, neck injury,
loss of consciousness, or delayed complications and requires urgent medical assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strength Testing} Manual muscle testing, dynamometry, isokinetic testing. Quantifies weakness, tracks
progress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strep Throat} Acute streptococcal pharyngitis caused by group A Streptococcus (S. pyogenes), most
common in children 5-15 years. Features: sudden sore throat, fever, tonsillar exudate,
palatal petechiae, anterior cervical lymphadenopathy, and headache/abdominal pain;
cough, coryza, and oral ulcers suggest viral.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Streptococcal cellulitis with lymphangitis} Streptococcal infection of the skin and subcutaneous tissue with inflammation
of the draining lymphatic vessels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Streptococcal Infection} Infections caused by Streptococcus pyogenes (Group A Strep, GAS). Common presentations:
pharyngitis (strep throat), scarlet fever (pharyngitis with erythematous rash),
impetigo, cellulitis, and necrotizing fasciitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Streptococcal pharyngitis} Sore throat caused by infection with group A Streptococcus, often accompanied by fever and
swollen cervical lymph nodes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Streptococcus agalactiae} A gram-positive, beta-hemolytic streptococcus (Group B Streptococcus) that is a leading
cause of neonatal sepsis, meningitis, and pneumonia. The organism colonises the
gastrointestinal and genital tracts. Transmission occurs during birth. Risk factors
include prematurity, prolonged rupture of membranes, and maternal colonisation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Streptococcus pneumoniae} A gram-positive, encapsulated diplococcus that causes pneumonia, otitis media,
sinusitis, meningitis, and sepsis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Streptococcus pyogenes} A gram-positive, beta-hemolytic streptococcus (Group A Streptococcus) that causes
pharyngitis, skin infections, and post-infectious complications including rheumatic
fever and glomerulonephritis. The organism produces streptolysins, erythrogenic toxin,
and streptokinase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Streptokinase} A thrombolytic agent that forms a plasminogen activator complex, converting plasminogen
