to plasmin and lysing fibrin clots. Used for STEMI, massive pulmonary embolism, and deep
vein thrombosis (largely replaced by tissue plasminogen activator, which is more
fibrin-specific and less antigenic).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress} A physiological and psychological response to perceived threats or demands that
activates the sympathetic nervous system and the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Cardiomyopathy} A temporary weakening of the heart muscle, often following intense emotional or physical stress,
with symptoms that can resemble acute coronary syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Echocardiography} Echocardiography during exercise or pharmacological stress. Detects coronary artery
disease by identifying wall motion abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Fracture} A bone injury caused by repetitive submaximal loading that exceeds the rate of
remodeling and repair. It often presents with gradually increasing, localized
activity-related pain and may progress to a complete fracture if loading continues.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Shielding} Reduction in physiologic loading of adjacent bone caused by an implant that carries a
disproportionate share of mechanical force. Chronic stress shielding may lead to bone
resorption and compromise fixation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Ulcer Prophylaxis} Prevention of stress-related mucosal disease in critically ill patients. Risk factors:
mechanical ventilation >48 hours, coagulopathy, trauma, burns, sepsis. Agents: H2
blockers, proton pump inhibitors. Duration: while risk factors present.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Urinary Incontinence} Involuntary leakage of urine, significantly affecting psychological, social, and
physical well-being. See urinary incontinence.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stretch Reflex} A monosynaptic spinal reflex in which stretching a muscle (e.g., a tap on the patellar
tendon) excites muscle spindles, generating an afferent signal (Ia fibers) that directly
excites the alpha motor neurons of the same muscle, causing contraction and resistance
to stretch.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Striae Gravidarum} Stretch marks that develop during pregnancy, commonly on the abdomen, breasts, thighs, or
buttocks. They may begin as red or purple lines and gradually fade to lighter bands.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stridor} Stridor concerns stridor; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
narrowed upper airway. Inspiratory stridor usually indicates supraglottic or glottic
obstruction; biphasic or expiratory patterns help localize disease, but any severe or
progressive stridor is an airway emergency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stripping of Membranes (Membrane Sweep)} A procedure performed during a cervical exam where the examiner inserts a finger through
the internal os and sweeps the membranes circumferentially, separating them from the
lower uterine segment. Releases endogenous prostaglandins, promoting cervical ripening
and potentially initiating labor.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Membrane Sweep

\medterm{Stroke} Stroke is sudden neurologic dysfunction from interrupted cerebral blood flow or bleeding, causing focal deficits and requiring urgent evaluation for reperfusion or hemorrhage management.
neuronal death and neurological deficits.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stroke Volume} The volume of blood ejected by the left ventricle with each heartbeat, normally 60-100
mL. It is determined by three factors: preload, afterload, and myocardial contractility.
Stroke volume equals end-diastolic volume minus end-systolic volume. Reduced stroke
volume occurs in heart failure, hypovolemia, and cardiogenic shock.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strongyloides stercoralis} A soil-transmitted nematode that can infect the human intestine and cause strongyloidiasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strongyloidiasis} Strongyloidiasis concerns strongyloidiasis; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
Most infections asymptomatic or mild (abdominal pain, diarrhea, urticaria). Larvae
migration causes larva currens (rapidly migrating serpiginous rash, perianal).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Structured Reporting} Reporting using standardized templates. Improves consistency, completeness.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Struvite Stones} Infection stones composed of magnesium ammonium phosphate. Form in alkaline urine with
urease-producing organisms including Proteus and Klebsiella. Staghorn calculi fill renal
pelvis almost exclusively from Proteus. Treatment requires complete stone removal with
antibiotics and urinary acidification. High recurrence if infection persists.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stupor} A state of reduced consciousness in which the patient is unresponsive to most
environmental stimuli but can be temporarily aroused by vigorous, repeated stimulation
(painful stimuli, loud shouting).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sturge-Weber Syndrome} A neurocutaneous syndrome (phakomatosis) characterized by a facial capillary
malformation (port-wine stain) in the V1 distribution of the trigeminal nerve (forehead
and upper eyelid) and ipsilateral leptomeningeal angiomatosis (venous malformations of
the pia mater). Caused by a somatic activating mutation in GNAQ.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stye} A common infection of the eyelid. External hordeolum: acute bacterial infection of the
Zeis or Moll glands at the eyelash follicle, typically caused by Staphylococcus aureus.
Presents as a tender, red, well-localized swelling at the lid margin. Internal
hordeolum: infection of the meibomian gland, larger and more painful than external.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subacute} Between acute and chronic. Examples: subacute thyroiditis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subacute Rehabilitation} Less intensive than acute rehab. For patients needing continued therapy but not as
intensive. Skilled nursing facility setting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subacute Thyroiditis} Inflammatory condition of the thyroid most likely viral in etiology presenting with
painful tender thyroid enlargement, fever, and malaise. Associated with elevated
erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subarachnoid} Situated beneath the arachnoid membrane surrounding the brain and spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subarachnoid Hemorrhage} Bleeding into the subarachnoid space, the area between the arachnoid membrane and the
pia mater surrounding the brain, most commonly caused by rupture of a cerebral (berry)
aneurysm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subclavian Artery} The artery supplying the upper limb, arising on the right from the brachiocephalic trunk
and on the left directly from the aortic arch, passing over the first rib and becoming
the axillary artery. Branches: vertebral, internal thoracic, thyrocervical trunk,
costocervical trunk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subclavian Vein} The continuation of the axillary vein beyond the lateral border of the first rib. It
joins the internal jugular vein behind the sternoclavicular joint to form the
brachiocephalic vein.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subclavian vein thrombosis} Formation of a blood clot in the subclavian vein, often associated with central venous
catheterization, thoracic outlet syndrome, or Paget-Schroetter syndrome (effort-related
thrombosis in young, active individuals). Presents with arm swelling, pain, and
cyanosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subcorneal pustular dermatosis} A chronic relapsing skin disorder characterized by superficial sterile pustules beneath the
stratum corneum, often on the trunk and flexural areas. It may be associated with IgA or
other monoclonal gammopathies and can resemble pustular psoriasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subculture} The transfer of microorganisms from one culture to fresh culture medium, used to
maintain pure cultures, isolate colonies, increase viability, prepare standardized
inocula, and perform identification/susceptibility testing. On solid media, subculture
aims to obtain isolated single colonies from a mixed or stock culture.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subcutaneous Emphysema} Air in the subcutaneous tissues, often from pneumothorax, pneumomediastinum, or chest
trauma. It causes a characteristic crepitus on palpation. It is usually benign and
resolves when the underlying air leak is treated. Severe cases may cause skin necrosis
and compartment syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subcutaneous Tissue} Subcutaneous tissue (hypodermis, superficial fascia) lies beneath the dermis and
connects skin to underlying structures. It contains adipose tissue (fat), blood vessels,
nerves, and the superficial muscular aponeurotic system (SMAS). Subcutaneous fat
provides insulation, cushioning, and energy storage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subdural Hematoma} A collection of blood between the dura mater and the arachnoid mater, caused by tearing
of bridging veins that traverse the subdural space.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subgaleal Hemorrhage} A potentially life-threatening accumulation of blood in the space beneath the scalp's
epicranial aponeurosis. It can expand rapidly in a newborn, particularly after vacuum-assisted delivery, and requires
urgent recognition and treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sublingual epidermoid cyst} A benign cyst lined by stratified squamous epithelium beneath the tongue mucosa,
presenting as a slow-growing midline swelling that may require surgical excision if
symptomatic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sublingual Gland} One of the paired major salivary glands, located beneath the tongue. It produces mainly mucous saliva that enters the
mouth through several small ducts and helps lubricate the oral cavity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Submandibular Gland} One of the three paired major salivary glands, located in the submandibular triangle
beneath the mandible. Mixed gland (predominantly serous) secreting saliva via Wharton's
duct opening at the sublingual caruncle (floor of mouth). Saliva contains amylase,
mucins, lysozyme, IgA.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subscapular Artery} An artery arising from the axillary artery that supplies the scapular region and
adjacent muscles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Abuse} An older, nonspecific term for harmful or problematic substance use. Current clinical language generally uses substance-use disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Abuse Psychiatry} The subspecialty focused on the evaluation and treatment of substance use disorders and
co-occurring psychiatric conditions. Dual diagnosis patients require integrated
treatment addressing both disorders simultaneously.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Dependence} A state in which repeated substance use produces physiologic adaptation, so that reducing or stopping the substance may cause withdrawal. It is distinct from, but may occur with, substance-use disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Intoxication} A reversible substance-specific syndrome caused by recent use that produces clinically
significant behavioral or physiologic changes. Diagnosis requires assessing timing, dose
or exposure, expected pharmacology, co-ingestants, and medical complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Use} The consumption of alcohol, drugs, or other chemical substances, which can lead to
health, social, and psychological issues if used excessively.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Use Disorder} A problematic pattern of substance use leading to clinically significant impairment or
distress, manifested by at least two of eleven criteria within a twelve-month period.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Use in Pregnancy} Use of alcohol, tobacco, cannabis, opioids, cocaine, or other substances during
pregnancy. Effects depend on the substance, dose, timing, and other factors and may
include maternal complications, impaired fetal growth, preterm birth, or neonatal
withdrawal. Evaluation and treatment should be individualized.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substance Withdrawal} A substance-specific syndrome occurring after reduction or cessation of heavy or
prolonged use. Withdrawal can range from discomfort to life-threatening complications
such as seizures, delirium tremens, severe autonomic instability, or respiratory
depression depending on the substance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Substernal goiter} Extension of thyroid tissue into the mediastinum, typically arising from the thyroid
gland in the neck and growing downward through the thoracic inlet. It may cause
compressive symptoms including dyspnea, dysphagia, and superior vena cava syndrome. Most
are multinodular goiters. Diagnosis is by CT or MRI.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Succenturial Lobe} An accessory lobe of the placenta, connected to the main placenta by blood vessels that
traverse the membranes. If undetected after delivery, the succenturial lobe may be
retained in the uterus, causing postpartum hemorrhage or infection. Diagnosis: careful
inspection of the placenta and membranes after delivery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Succinate Dehydrogenase} Complex II of electron transport chain. Also TCA cycle enzyme. Produces FADH2.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Succinylcholine} A depolarizing neuromuscular-blocking drug that produces rapid, brief skeletal-muscle
paralysis by activating nicotinic acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction.
It can cause hyperkalemia, bradycardia, myalgia, masseter spasm, prolonged apnea in
pseudocholinesterase deficiency, and malignant hyperthermia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sucking Reflex} A primitive reflex present from birth, elicited by placing an object in the infant's
mouth. The infant rhythmically sucks on the object. Coordinated sucking and swallowing
typically develop by 34-36 weeks gestational age. Premature infants may lack coordinated
suck-swallow, requiring gavage feeding.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sucrose Lysis Test} A historical screening test for paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria in which patient
red blood cells are exposed to serum in a low-ionic-strength sucrose solution, allowing
complement-mediated lysis to be detected.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suction (Vascular)} A suction device used during a vascular procedure to remove blood or other fluid from the operative
field.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Vascular

\medterm{Suction Device} Creates negative pressure for fluid removal. Components: canister, tubing, suction
catheter. Used for airway clearance, surgical field, wound drainage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suction Tip} The end attachment of a suction device used to remove blood, fluid, secretions, or debris.
Common types include the Yankauer tip for oropharyngeal suction and surgical suction tips such as the Frink tip.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sudden Cardiac Death} Unexpected death from a cardiac cause, usually occurring rapidly after symptom onset or without
witnessed symptoms. Causes include coronary disease, cardiomyopathy, inherited arrhythmia
syndromes, and bradyarrhythmia; prevention depends on the underlying risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sudden Infant Death Syndrome} SIDS: the sudden, unexplained death of an infant <1 year (peak 2-4 months) that remains
unexplained after a thorough investigation (autopsy, scene examination, and review of
clinical history). Risk factors: prone sleep, bed-sharing, soft bedding, overheating,
maternal smoking, prematurity/low birth weight, and co-sleeping.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sudden Sensorineural Hearing Loss} Rapid sensorineural hearing loss, conventionally at least 30 dB over three contiguous
frequencies within 72 hours. It requires urgent audiologic and otologic assessment
because early treatment may improve recovery in selected patients.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sudden Unexpected Infant Death} The sudden and unexpected death of an infant younger than 1 year, whether or not the
cause is known before investigation. Sudden infant death syndrome is the unexplained
subtype after complete scene, history, and autopsy evaluation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suffocation} Death or injury from failure of oxygen to reach the lungs, a category of asphyxia
distinct from strangulation. Subtypes include smothering (airway obstruction), choking
or foreign-body obstruction, mechanical asphyxia (positional, chest compression), and
entrapment in oxygen-deficient environments.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sugammadex} A selective relaxant binding agent that reverses aminosteroid neuromuscular blockers
(rocuronium and vecuronium) by encapsulating them in a cyclodextrin molecule, forming a
1:1 complex that is renally excreted. Onset of action is within 2 to 3 minutes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suicidal Ideation} Thoughts about killing oneself, ranging from passive (wish to be dead, wishes to fall
asleep and not wake) to active (thoughts of killing oneself with varying degrees of
intent, plan, and means). The C-SSRS grades severity from 1 (wish to be dead) to 5
(active suicidal ideation with specific plan and intent).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suicide} The intentional act of taking one's own life. Suicide is a major public health problem
worldwide, causing ~700,000 deaths annually.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suicide Risk Assessment} A clinical evaluation of suicidal thoughts, intent, plan, access to means, past
attempts, self-harm, psychiatric and medical illness, substance use, protective factors,
and immediate safety. It informs but does not replace ongoing clinical judgment and
safety planning.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sulfa Drugs} A class of bacteriostatic antibiotics that competitively inhibit dihydropteroate
synthase, an enzyme essential for folic acid synthesis in bacteria. See sulfonamides.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sulfasalazine} A DMARD combining sulfapyridine and 5-aminosalicylic acid, used in rheumatoid arthritis,
psoriatic arthritis, and inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis). Slows disease
progression in RA; onset over 1-3 months.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sulfatide} A sulfated glycolipid found particularly in myelin and nervous tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sulfonamide} A class of antibiotics (sulfamethoxazole, sulfadiazine, sulfasalazine) that inhibit
bacterial dihydropteroate synthase, blocking folate synthesis—bacteriostatic. Often
combined with trimethoprim (co-trimoxazole: TMP-SMX). Used for UTIs, PCP (pneumocystis
pneumonia), toxoplasmosis, Nocardia, and MRSA.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sulfonylureas} Oral antidiabetic agents that stimulate insulin secretion from pancreatic beta cells by
blocking ATP-sensitive potassium channels on the beta cell membrane. This causes
membrane depolarization, opening of voltage-gated calcium channels, calcium influx, and
exocytosis of insulin-containing granules.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sulfur Colloid Scan} Nuclear medicine imaging using Tc-99m sulfur colloid. Evaluates liver, spleen, bone
marrow, lymph nodes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sulphonamides} A class of antimicrobial agents containing the sulfonamide group (SO2NH2).
Sulphonamides were the first effective systemic antibacterial agents (Prontosil,
1935—Gerhard Domagk). Mechanism: competitive inhibition of dihydropteroate synthase,
blocking folate synthesis (para-aminobenzoic acid analogue)—bacteriostatic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sun Protection (Photoprotection)} Sun protection involves measures taken to reduce exposure to ultraviolet radiation from
the sun, which is the primary environmental risk factor for skin cancer and photoaging.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Photoprotection

\medterm{Sunburn} Sunburn concerns sunburn; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
ultraviolet (UV) radiation (UVB predominantly), causing DNA damage and inflammatory
response. Risk: repeated sunburn increases skin cancer risk (melanoma, basal/squamous
cell carcinoma) and photoaging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superantigen} A protein that bypasses antigen processing and non-specifically activates large T-cell
populations by binding MHC class II and the T-cell receptor V region, triggering massive
cytokine release. Staphylococcal TSST-1 and enterotoxins, and streptococcal pyrogenic
exotoxins, cause toxic shock and scarlet fever.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superficial} Located near or on the surface of the body.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superficial Fascia} A layer of loose connective tissue beneath the dermis, also called subcutaneous tissue
or the hypodermis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
subcutaneous tissue.

\medterm{Superficial thrombophlebitis} Superficial thrombophlebitis is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
along the vein.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superimposed Preeclampsia} Preeclampsia developing in a person who already has chronic hypertension. It may
present with new or worsening proteinuria, thrombocytopenia, abnormal liver or kidney tests, neurologic symptoms, or
other features of maternal organ dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superinfection} A new infection that develops during or after treatment of a previous infection, often
involving an antimicrobial-resistant organism or an organism not susceptible to the
original therapy, such as Clostridioides difficile after antibiotic exposure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superior} Positioned higher or closer to the head.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superior Mesenteric Artery} Second major abdominal aortic branch at L1, the midgut artery. Supplies distal duodenum
through proximal two-thirds of transverse colon. Branches: inferior pancreaticoduodenal,
jejunal, ileal, ileocolic, right colic, middle colic. Arises anterior to the pancreatic
uncinate process, crosses the third duodenal part.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superior mesenteric artery syndrome} Compression of the duodenum between the aorta and the superior mesenteric artery,
causing proximal duodenal obstruction. Presents with postprandial nausea, vomiting, and
epigastric pain, often after significant weight loss that narrows the SMA-aortic angle.
Treatment includes nutritional rehabilitation and surgical duodenal mobilization.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superior Semicircular Canal Dehiscence} A defect or marked thinning of the bony covering of the superior semicircular canal that
can produce sound- or pressure-induced vertigo, autophony, and conductive-appearing
hearing findings. Diagnosis combines characteristic symptoms, physiologic testing, and
high-resolution temporal-bone CT.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superior Vena Cava} Formed by brachiocephalic vein junction at the first right costal cartilage, returning
upper body deoxygenated blood to the right atrium. Approximately 7 cm, valveless.
Related to right lung/pleura, right phrenic nerve, and azygos vein.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superior Vena Cava Syndrome} Obstruction of SVC by tumor. Facial swelling, dyspnea, dilated neck veins. Emergency:
radiation, stenting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Superior vena caval obstruction} Compression or thrombosis of the superior vena cava impairing venous return from the
head, neck, and upper extremities. Most commonly caused by malignancy (lung cancer,
lymphoma) or indwelling central venous devices. Presents with facial swelling, plethora,
distended neck veins, and upper extremity edema.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supernumerary rib} An extra rib beyond the typical twelve pairs, most commonly a cervical rib above the
first thoracic rib, which may compress the brachial plexus or subclavian vessel causing
thoracic outlet syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supernumerary Teeth} Extra teeth beyond normal complement. Most common: mesiodens between maxillary centrals.
May cause impaction or malocclusion.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supervised Exercise Therapy} A structured, monitored exercise program used to improve walking ability and
reduce claudication symptoms in people with peripheral artery disease. The exercise plan
is adjusted to the person’s symptoms and functional capacity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supination} Rotation of the forearm so that the palm faces anteriorly in anatomical position, or
upward when the elbow is flexed. The radius and ulna become parallel.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supine Hypotensive Syndrome} Compression of the inferior vena cava by the gravid uterus when the pregnant woman lies
flat on her back after 20 weeks gestation. Results in decreased venous return, reduced
cardiac output, and maternal hypotension, dizziness, pallor, and syncope.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supplemental Oxygen Therapy} Administration of oxygen at a concentration above that of room air to treat or prevent
clinically important hypoxemia. Delivery method and oxygen target depend on the illness,
baseline oxygenation, risk of carbon-dioxide retention, and monitoring results; excessive
oxygen can also cause harm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supplementation} Use of a vitamin, mineral, protein, fiber, or other nutrient preparation to correct a
deficiency or meet a clinically identified increased requirement. The indication, dose,
interactions, product quality, and risk of toxicity should be assessed before use.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supportive Care} Treatment that maintains essential body functions, relieves symptoms, prevents complications, and supports recovery while the underlying cause is treated.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suppository} A solid, single-dose dosage form designed to be inserted into a body orifice (rectum,
vagina, or urethra) where it melts, softens, or dissolves, releasing the active
pharmaceutical ingredient for local or systemic effects.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supraglottic Airway Device} An airway device positioned above the glottis to maintain oxygenation and ventilation
without passing through the vocal cords. It is used for selected anesthetized or
unconscious patients and is not a definitive substitute for a cuffed tracheal tube when
aspiration protection is required.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suprascapular Nerve} A branch of the brachial plexus that supplies the supraspinatus and infraspinatus
muscles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supraventricular Tachycardia} A rapid rhythm (150-250 bpm) originating above the ventricles, characterized by narrow
QRS complexes. Most common forms are AV nodal reentrant tachycardia (AVNRT) and AV
reentrant tachycardia (AVRT). Episodes are typically paroxysmal with sudden onset and
offset. Vagal maneuvers and adenosine are diagnostic and therapeutic.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surfactant} A phospholipid-protein complex produced by type II pneumocytes that reduces alveolar
surface tension, preventing atelectasis. Deficiency causes respiratory distress syndrome
in premature infants. Exogenous surfactant (beractant, poractant alfa) administered via
endotracheal tube.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgery} Treatment in which an operation is performed to diagnose, remove, repair, reconstruct, or otherwise treat a disease or injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Drain} A device placed to remove blood, pus, bile, urine, lymph, or other fluid from a surgical
site or body cavity. Indications, output characteristics, duration, and removal criteria
should be documented because drains can also cause infection, bleeding, or tissue
injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Hemostasis} Control of bleeding during an operation using pressure, ligation, sutures, clips,
electrosurgery, topical agents, vessel sealing, or other techniques. Effective
hemostasis requires identification of the bleeding source and correction of coagulopathy
when present.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Instruments} Instruments for surgery. Categories: cutting (scalpel, scissors), grasping (forceps,
clamps), retraction, hemostasis (hemostat, electrocautery).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Margin} The edge of tissue removed during an excision or resection, assessed microscopically for
residual disease. A negative margin has no tumor at the examined edge; a positive margin
indicates tumor extends to the inked or designated margin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Repair} An operation that restores or reconstructs damaged tissue, an organ, or an anatomic function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Risk Stratification} Estimation of the likelihood of perioperative complications using patient factors,
urgency, procedure risk, functional capacity, and disease-specific assessment. It
informs optimization, monitoring level, shared decisions, and postoperative planning but
cannot eliminate uncertainty.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Safety Checklist} A structured checklist used at defined points before anesthesia, before incision, and
before leaving the operating room to verify patient identity, procedure, site, consent,
equipment, specimens, and team communication.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Site Infection} Infection involving the incision, organ, or operative space within the surveillance
period after surgery. It may be superficial incisional, deep incisional, or organ-space
and requires appropriate culture, antimicrobial therapy, drainage, debridement, or
reoperation when indicated.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Time-Out} A deliberate team pause immediately before incision to verify patient identity,
procedure, site, consent, allergies, antibiotics, imaging, implants, equipment, and
anticipated critical events. It is a core surgical-safety intervention.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Wound Classification} Categorization of an operative wound as clean, clean-contaminated, contaminated, or
dirty/infected according to the degree of microbial exposure and existing infection. The
class helps estimate surgical-site infection risk and guide prophylaxis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surrogate Decision-Maker} A person authorized to make healthcare decisions for someone who lacks decision-making capacity, guided by the person's
known values and preferences.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surveillance} Systematic collection, analysis, interpretation of health data. Types: passive
(reporting), active (proactive case finding), sentinel (selected sites).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surveillance Imaging} Repeat imaging to detect recurrence. Protocol varies by cancer type, stage, treatment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Survival Rate} Percentage of patients surviving for specified time after diagnosis. 5-year survival
commonly used.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Survivorship} Care for cancer survivors. Addresses late effects, surveillance, quality of life.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Survivorship Care Plan} Document summarizing treatment, follow-up, late effects. Provided to cancer survivors.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Susac Syndrome} An autoimmune microangiopathy affecting the brain, retina, and inner ear. The
characteristic triad is encephalopathy, branch retinal-artery occlusions, and
sensorineural hearing loss, though all features may not be present initially.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Susceptibility Testing} Laboratory tests determining the sensitivity of a bacterial isolate to antimicrobials to
guide therapy. Methods: disk diffusion (Kirby-Bauer—zone sizes), broth dilution
(MIC—minimum inhibitory concentration, MBC—minimum bactericidal concentration), E-test
(gradient strips), and automated systems (VITEK).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suspected Cause} A condition or factor believed to explain a disease or symptom but not yet confirmed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suspension} A pharmaceutical formulation in which fine solid drug particles are dispersed (not
dissolved) in a liquid vehicle, often with suspending agents and stabilizers.
Suspensions are used when a drug is poorly soluble, for oral (antibiotic suspensions,
antacids), topical, ophthalmic, and parenteral (depot) administration.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suture} A thread or strand of material used to approximate tissues (bring wound edges together)
and hold them in place until healing occurs.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suture (Perineal)} A thread or strand of material used to approximate tissues (bring wound edges together)
and hold them in place until healing occurs. See Suture.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Perineal

\medterm{Suture Anchor} A small implant that secures a tendon, ligament, or other soft tissue to bone. It may be
made from metal, polymer, or bioabsorbable material and can fail through pullout,
breakage, migration, or inflammatory reaction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SUV} Standardized uptake value, a semiquantitative measure of radiotracer concentration used mainly in PET imaging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{SVC Syndrome} Obstruction of the superior vena cava causing facial swelling, plethora, and distended
neck veins. Commonly from lung cancer (SCLC, squamous) and lymphoma. Oncologic
emergency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Swallow Study} X-ray imaging of swallowing using barium. Evaluates dysphagia, aspiration, structural
abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Swallowing} Difficulty swallowing, classified as oropharyngeal (difficulty initiating a swallow,
choking, coughing, nasal regurgitation) or esophageal (sensation of food sticking
retrosternally). See dysphagia.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Swallowing Assessment} Clinical and, when indicated, instrumental evaluation of oral, pharyngeal, and
esophageal swallowing to identify dysphagia, aspiration, and appropriate food or liquid
consistency. Videofluoroscopic or endoscopic studies may be required when bedside
assessment is insufficient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Swallowing Therapy} Assessment and treatment to improve the safety and efficiency of swallowing and reduce aspiration risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sweat Chloride Test} A test that measures chloride concentration in sweat after pilocarpine stimulation. It is an
important diagnostic test for cystic fibrosis, but results must be interpreted with the
clinical findings and, when needed, genetic or other testing. Some illnesses and technical
factors can produce misleading results.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sweat Gland} A skin gland that produces sweat, which contributes mainly to temperature regulation and skin surface function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sweating} Sweating concerns sweating; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
controlled by sympathetic cholinergic innervation (in contrast to the catecholaminergic
sympathetic response elsewhere).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sweet Syndrome} An inflammatory skin disorder characterized by sudden fever and tender red or violaceous plaques or nodules containing
many neutrophils. It may be associated with infection, medicines, inflammatory disease,
or cancer.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sweet's syndrome} Acute febrile neutrophilic dermatosis, characterized by fever, neutrophilia, and
painful, erythematous plaques or nodules on the skin, histologically showing dense
neutrophilic infiltration of the dermis. It may be idiopathic, drug-induced, or
associated with malignancy, particularly hematological cancers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Swine Flu} A contagious respiratory illness caused by influenza A or B viruses, characterized by
sudden onset of fever, myalgia, headache, malaise, nonproductive cough, sore throat, and
nasal congestion. See Influenza.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Swollen Ankles and Swollen Feet} Enlargement of the ankles or feet caused by injury, venous insufficiency, lymphatic disease, medication,
pregnancy, or systemic fluid retention from heart, kidney, or liver disease. Sudden one-sided swelling with pain,
redness, warmth, or breathlessness may indicate thrombosis or infection and requires urgent assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Symblepharon} Adhesion of the palpebral conjunctiva (inner eyelid) to the bulbar conjunctiva or
cornea, most commonly resulting from chemical burns, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, ocular
cicatricial pemphigoid, or repeated surgical intervention. It restricts eye movement and
may require surgical lysis with amniotic membrane or mucous membrane grafting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sympathetic Nervous System} The division of the autonomic nervous system responsible for the 'fight or flight'
response, preparing the body for intense physical activity or stress. Sympathetic
preganglionic neurons originate in the intermediolateral column of the spinal cord gray
matter (T1--L2), making it the thoracolumbar system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sympatholytic} An agent that blocks the sympathetic nervous system, used to treat hypertension,
sympathetic overactivity, and catecholamine excess. Classes: beta-blockers (propranolol,
metoprolol), alpha-blockers (prazosin, doxazosin, phenoxybenzamine), centrally acting
agents (clonidine, methyldopa), and neuron blockers (reserpine, guanethidine).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Symptom} Subjective experience of disease. Examples: pain, fatigue, nausea. Reported by patient.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synapse} The specialized junction between a neuron and another neuron (or effector cell—muscle,
gland) where information is transmitted from one cell to another.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synaptic Plasticity} Activity-dependent change in the strength or structure of synaptic transmission.
Long-term potentiation and long-term depression are important cellular mechanisms
underlying learning, memory, adaptation, and some forms of chronic pain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synaptic Transmission} The fundamental process by which neurons communicate with each other through specialized
junctions called synapses.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syncope} A transient loss of consciousness caused by temporary global cerebral hypoperfusion. See Fainting.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syncope (Fainting)} A transient loss of consciousness caused by temporary global cerebral hypoperfusion. See Fainting.



\textbf{Synonyms}:
Fainting

\medterm{Syndactyly} A congenital connection of two or more adjacent digits by skin, and sometimes by bone or other tissues.
It may occur alone or as part of a genetic syndrome; treatment depends on function, anatomy,
and associated abnormalities.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syndrome} Group of symptoms and signs occurring together. Examples: metabolic syndrome, irritable
bowel syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syndrome of inappropriate ADH secretion} Excessive antidiuretic hormone activity causing euvolemic hyponatremia with
inappropriately concentrated urine and elevated urinary sodium.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syndrome Surveillance} The public health monitoring of pre-diagnostic clinical indicators (symptoms,
syndromes—fever, rash, GI illness, influenza-like illness) in real time to detect
outbreaks, bioterrorism, or emerging infections earlier than laboratory confirmation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syndromic Surveillance} Monitoring of symptoms, clinical encounters, laboratory requests, or other
near-real-time signals before definitive diagnoses are available. It supports early
detection of outbreaks and public-health threats but may have lower specificity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovial chondromatosis} A benign, metaplastic condition characterized by the formation of multiple cartilaginous
loose bodies within joint synovium, bursae, or tendon sheaths. It presents with joint
pain, swelling, locking, and restricted range of motion, most commonly affecting the
knee, hip, and elbow.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovial Fluid Analysis} Laboratory examination of aspirated joint fluid for appearance, viscosity, leukocyte
count and differential, crystals under polarized light, Gram stain, and culture. It
helps distinguish noninflammatory, inflammatory, crystal-associated, and septic
arthritis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovial Sarcoma} A soft tissue sarcoma typically arising near joints of the extremities in young adults,
characterized by the t(X;18) chromosomal translocation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovitis} Inflammation of the synovial membrane lining a joint, causing swelling, pain, warmth,
and joint effusion. Causes: inflammatory arthritis (RA, psoriatic, gout, reactive,
septic), autoimmune (SLE), and trauma/overuse.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovium} The inner layer of the joint capsule in synovial joints, composed of synovial lining
cells (type A macrophage-like, type B fibroblast-like) on a vascularized connective
tissue base. Produces synovial fluid (hyaluronic acid, lubricin, proteinases) for
lubrication and chondrocyte nutrition.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovography} Contrast imaging of joint cavity. Evaluates synovial pathology.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synthesis Module} Automated system for radiopharmaceutical preparation. PET tracer synthesis: FDG,
Florbetapir. Quality control: radiochemical purity, pH, sterility.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syphilis} Syphilis concerns syphilis; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.
latent, and tertiary stages, although the stages can overlap or be unrecognized.
Diagnosis uses clinical assessment and serologic testing. Treatment depends on the stage
and site of infection; penicillin is used in many cases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syphilitic alopecia} Non-scarring hair loss occurring as a manifestation of secondary syphilis, presenting
with diffuse thinning or patchy, moth-eaten alopecia of the scalp, eyebrows, or beard.
It is thought to result from direct spirochaetal invasion of hair follicles during the
spirochaetaemic phase.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syphilitic gumma} A destructive, granulomatous lesion occurring in tertiary syphilis, characterized by a
center of necrotic tissue surrounded by plasma cells, lymphocytes, and fibroblasts.
Gummas may affect any organ, most commonly the skin, bones, liver, and central nervous
system. They present as painless, firm nodules that may ulcerate.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syringe} Cylindrical device for injecting or withdrawing fluids. Components: barrel, plunger,
luer lock. Various sizes: 1 mL to 60 mL.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syringomyelia} Syringomyelia is a disorder affecting the body system or process named by the term. Clinical features and causes vary, so diagnosis is based on the history, examination, and appropriate investigations.
spinal cord, which can expand and elongate over time, compressing and destroying the
surrounding spinal cord tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syrinx} A syrinx is a fluid-filled cavity within the spinal cord, typically containing
cerebrospinal fluid or a similar fluid, that develops as a result of abnormal CSF flow
dynamics within the spinal cord. Syringomyelia: a syrinx in the central spinal cord.
Hydromyelia: dilation of the central canal.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systematic Review} Comprehensive summary of research on specific question. Uses explicit methods to
minimize bias. Cochrane reviews example.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Antifungals} Antifungal medicines taken orally or given intravenously for infections that are extensive,
invasive, refractory, or unsuitable for topical treatment. Choice and duration depend on the
organism, site, severity, drug interactions, organ function, and local resistance patterns.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Effects} Whole-body effects of toxins. Cardiac, neurological, renal, hepatic, hematological.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Inflammatory Response Syndrome} A systemic inflammatory response that may occur with infection, trauma, burns,
pancreatitis, surgery, or other insults. It can involve abnormal temperature, heart rate,
respiratory rate, or white-cell count. Modern sepsis assessment uses broader clinical
criteria rather than SIRS alone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis} A subtype of juvenile idiopathic arthritis with prominent systemic
inflammation—daily spiking fevers, evanescent salmon-pink rash, arthritis,
lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, and elevated inflammatory markers (ferritin).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Lupus Erythematosus} Multisystem autoimmune disease, more common in women. Features: malar rash,
photosensitivity, oral ulcers, arthritis, serositis, renal disorder, neurologic
disorder, hematologic disorder, immunologic disorder. Diagnosis: ANA positive plus
clinical/immunologic criteria.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Sclerosis} Systemic sclerosis, also known as scleroderma, is a chronic autoimmune disease
characterized by progressive fibrosis of the skin and internal organs, vasculopathy, and
immune dysfunction.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
scleroderma, is a chronic autoimmune disease.

\medterm{Systemic Therapy} Treatment that reaches the whole body, including chemotherapy, endocrine therapy,
targeted therapy, and immunotherapy, as opposed to local therapies such as surgery and
radiation.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Vascular Resistance} The resistance to blood flow offered by the systemic circulation, determined mainly by
arteriolar tone. It is calculated from the pressure gradient across the systemic
circulation and cardiac output and is a major determinant of arterial pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systolic Murmurs} Murmurs occurring between S1 and S2. Ejection systolic murmurs (crescendo-decrescendo)
are caused by flow across semilunar valves (aortic stenosis, pulmonary stenosis) or
increased flow across normal valves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sézary Syndrome} The leukemic variant of cutaneous T-cell lymphoma, characterized by erythroderma,
lymphadenopathy, pruritus, and circulating atypical lymphocytes (Sézary cells).

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sclerosing Peritonitis} Thickening and scarring of the peritoneum that can restrict movement of the intestines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scheuermann's Disease} A condition in adolescents that causes abnormal rounding of the upper back because of vertebral growth changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sjogren's Syndrome} An autoimmune disease that commonly causes dry eyes and dry mouth by damaging tear- and saliva-producing glands.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staghorn Calculi} Large branching kidney stones that fill part or all of the renal collecting system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sunstroke} A life-threatening form of heat illness in which body temperature rises dangerously high and the nervous system is impaired.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Mastocytosis} A disorder in which excessive mast cells accumulate in one or more organs and release inflammatory chemicals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Semi-Fowler Position} A semiupright position in which the head and upper body are elevated about 30 to 45 degrees.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sitting Position} An upright or semiupright position in which the patient's torso is elevated and the hips and knees are flexed; it is used for selected shoulder and neurosurgical procedures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Supine Position} A position in which the patient lies on the back with the face upward; it is the most commonly used surgical position.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sacral Dimple} A small indentation in the skin over the lower spine that is present at birth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salmonella Infection} An intestinal infection caused by Salmonella bacteria, usually acquired through contaminated food or water.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schwannomatosis} A disorder in which multiple schwannomas, tumors of nerve-sheath cells, develop.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sclerosing Mesenteritis} A rare inflammatory and fibrotic disorder affecting the mesentery, the tissue supporting the intestines.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scorpion Sting} A venomous injury caused by a scorpion, producing local pain and sometimes systemic toxicity.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scrotal Masses} Abnormal lumps or swellings in the tissues of the scrotum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sebaceous Carcinoma} An uncommon skin cancer arising from oil-producing sebaceous glands.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Secondary Hypertension} High blood pressure caused by another medical condition or by a medication.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Selective IgA Deficiency} An immune disorder characterized by very low or absent immunoglobulin A levels.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sex Headaches} Headaches triggered by sexual activity, including orgasm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sexually Transmitted Diseases} Infections transmitted primarily through sexual contact; also called sexually transmitted infections.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
sexually transmitted infections.

\medterm{Sezary Syndrome} An uncommon form of cutaneous T-cell lymphoma involving widespread red skin and abnormal lymphocytes in the blood.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinus Rhythm} The normal heart rhythm generated by electrical impulses from the sinoatrial node.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suppuration} Formation or discharge of pus during an inflammatory or infectious process.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Drill} A powered instrument used to cut, shape, or bore bone during surgery.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Stapler} A device that places metal or absorbable staples to close skin, tissue, or a hollow organ.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Schwann Cells} Glial cells of the peripheral nervous system that form myelin around many axons and support the repair and maintenance of peripheral nerves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Scintigraphy} Nuclear medicine imaging in which a radiotracer is administered and its distribution is recorded to evaluate the structure or function of an organ.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Senile Lentigines} Flat, sharply defined brown or tan spots caused by chronic ultraviolet exposure, commonly appearing on sun-exposed skin with age.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sensory Neuron} A nerve cell that carries information from sensory receptors toward the central nervous system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sensory Receptors} Specialized cells or nerve endings that detect stimuli such as touch, temperature, pain, light, sound, or chemical changes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sentinel Node Biopsy} Removal and examination of the first lymph node or nodes to which a tumor is likely to spread, used to assess regional metastasis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septal Defect} An abnormal opening in the septum between the heart's chambers that permits blood to pass between the right and left sides of the heart.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Seroconversion} The development of detectable antibodies in the blood after infection, vaccination, or another immune stimulus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sella Turcica} A saddle-shaped depression in the sphenoid bone that houses the pituitary gland.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Semilunar Valves} The aortic and pulmonary valves, which open during ventricular contraction and close during ventricular relaxation to prevent backflow into the ventricles.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Single-Photon Emission Computed Tomography} A nuclear medicine imaging technique that detects gamma rays from an injected radiotracer to create cross-sectional images of organ function and blood flow.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Simmonds Disease} An uncommon form of hypopituitarism caused by inadequate pituitary hormone production, often resulting in fatigue, weight loss, low blood pressure, and impaired endocrine function.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Turgor} The skin's ability to return to its normal position after being gently pinched. Reduced turgor may occur with dehydration, although it is less reliable in older adults.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Slit-Lamp Biomicroscope} An instrument that provides a magnified, illuminated view of the eye, especially the cornea, anterior chamber, iris, lens, and conjunctiva.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Snellen Chart} An eye chart used to measure distance visual acuity by testing the smallest line of letters a person can identify at a standard distance.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatoform Disorders} A former term for disorders involving prominent physical symptoms or health concerns associated with significant psychological distress. Current classifications use more specific diagnoses, such as somatic symptom disorder.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somatotropin} Growth hormone, a pituitary hormone that promotes growth and influences protein, fat, and carbohydrate metabolism.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Somnambulism} Sleepwalking; a parasomnia involving complex behaviors that arise during partial arousal from non-rapid-eye-movement sleep.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sound} A mechanical wave produced by vibration and transmitted through a medium. Hearing depends on its frequency, intensity, and processing by the auditory system.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spermatic Cord} A bundle extending from the abdomen to the testis that contains the vas deferens, testicular vessels, lymphatic vessels, nerves, and associated connective tissue.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spermatozoa} Mature male reproductive cells, commonly called sperm, that carry genetic material and can fertilize an oocyte.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
sperm, that carry genetic material and can fertilize an oocyte.

\medterm{Spermatozoon} A single mature male reproductive cell, or sperm cell.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sphincter of Oddi} A muscular valve controlling the flow of bile and pancreatic secretions into the duodenum.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spinal Cavity} The posterior body cavity formed by the vertebral column that contains and protects the spinal cord.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spiral Organ} The organ of Corti, a sensory structure in the cochlea containing hair cells that convert sound vibrations into nerve signals.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spirogram} A graphical record of air volumes and flow during pulmonary function testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spirometer} An instrument that measures the volume and flow of air inhaled and exhaled during pulmonary function testing.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Splenic Flexure} The bend of the colon where the transverse colon becomes the descending colon, near the spleen.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Split-Thickness Skin Graft} A graft containing the epidermis and part of the dermis, used to cover wounds or burns and allowing the donor site to heal from remaining skin structures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spondee} A two-syllable test word with equal emphasis on both syllables, used in speech audiometry.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spondylolysis} A stress fracture or defect in the pars interarticularis of a vertebral arch, most often in the lower lumbar spine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Spondylosis} Degenerative changes of the spine, including disc degeneration and bony overgrowth, commonly associated with aging.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staging} Assessment of the extent of cancer, including the size of the primary tumor, lymph-node involvement, and distant metastases.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stapedius} A small middle-ear muscle that helps limit movement of the stapes and may protect the inner ear from loud sounds.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stapling} Closure or joining of tissue with surgical staples.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stasis} Slowing or stopping of the normal flow of a body fluid, such as blood, lymph, or urine.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sterility} The inability to reproduce. In clinical use, the term may refer to infertility or to the absence of viable microorganisms, depending on context.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Steroid Hormones} Lipid-soluble hormones derived from cholesterol, including cortisol, aldosterone, estrogen, progesterone, and testosterone.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stimulants} Stimulants concerns stimulants; its medical significance is established from the defining features, setting, associated findings, and clinical question.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Incontinence} Involuntary leakage of urine during activities that increase intra-abdominal pressure, such as coughing, sneezing, lifting, or exercise.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Injuries} Tissue injuries caused by repeated loading or overuse without sufficient recovery. Examples include stress fractures, tendinopathy, and muscle strain.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stress Tests} Diagnostic tests that assess organ function under controlled physical, pharmacologic, or physiologic stress, commonly evaluating the heart for inducible ischemia or abnormal rhythm.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Striae Atrophicae} Linear bands of thin, wrinkled skin caused by stretching and disruption of dermal connective tissue; commonly called stretch marks.

\textbf{Synonyms}:
stretch marks.

\medterm{Student's Elbow} Olecranon bursitis, an inflammation of the bursa over the point of the elbow, often related to repeated pressure or trauma.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subacute Bacterial Endocarditis} A slowly progressive infection of the endocardium or heart valves, usually caused by less-virulent bacteria and often involving previously abnormal valves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subcutaneous Layer} The layer of tissue beneath the dermis, composed mainly of fat and connective tissue. It insulates the body, stores energy, and contains larger vessels and nerves.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subluxation} A partial loss of normal contact between the articular surfaces of a joint.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Surgical Fixation} Stabilization of a bone, joint, or other structure with devices such as plates, screws, rods, wires, or external frames.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Swan-Ganz Catheter} A pulmonary artery catheter used to measure pressures in the right heart and pulmonary artery and, when indicated, estimate cardiac output and pulmonary capillary wedge pressure.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sympathectomy} Surgical interruption or destruction of sympathetic nerves to reduce abnormal vasoconstriction, sweating, or pain in selected conditions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Symphysis} A cartilaginous joint in which two bones are united by fibrocartilage, such as the pubic symphysis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Symphysis Pubis} The fibrocartilaginous joint connecting the left and right pubic bones at the front of the pelvis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synechia} An abnormal adhesion between adjacent tissues, especially between parts of the iris or between the iris and the lens or cornea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synergist} A muscle that assists the prime mover during a movement or helps stabilize a joint.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovial Fluid} A viscous fluid within a synovial joint that lubricates articular surfaces and helps nourish cartilage.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Synovial Joint} A freely movable joint in which the articulating bones are separated by a fluid-filled joint cavity enclosed by a capsule.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systemic Circuit} The circulation that carries oxygenated blood from the left ventricle through the body and returns deoxygenated blood to the right atrium.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Systole} The phase of the cardiac cycle during which the ventricles contract and eject blood into the pulmonary artery and aorta.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Suprapubic} Located above or immediately behind the pubic bone, especially in reference to the lower abdominal or urinary-bladder region.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Salter-Harris Fracture} A fracture involving the growth plate of a child or adolescent. Types I through V describe progressively different patterns and risks to bone growth.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sternotomy} Surgical division of the sternum, usually along the midline, to provide access to the heart or mediastinal structures.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Telangiectasia} A visible, permanent dilation of small superficial blood vessels, appearing as fine red lines or web-like marks on the skin or mucous membranes.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serrated Polyposis Syndrome} A disorder characterized by multiple serrated lesions or polyps in the colon and an increased risk of colorectal cancer, requiring colonoscopic surveillance and removal of significant lesions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Staphylococcal Scalded Skin Syndrome} A toxin-mediated skin disorder caused by Staphylococcus aureus, usually affecting infants or young children and producing widespread superficial blistering and peeling of the skin.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Stent Restenosis} Re-narrowing of a blood vessel within or adjacent to a previously placed stent, usually caused by tissue growth, recurrent atherosclerosis, or thrombosis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinus Tachycardia} A heart rhythm originating in the sinoatrial node with a rate faster than normal, commonly occurring as a physiologic response to exercise, fever, pain, dehydration, or stress.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Status Migrainosus} A severe migraine attack that persists for more than 72 hours and may require urgent medical treatment, especially when dehydration, vomiting, or medication overuse is present.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subclinical Hypothyroidism} A condition with an elevated thyroid-stimulating hormone level and normal circulating free thyroid-hormone levels, often without symptoms or with only mild nonspecific symptoms.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Syndrome of Inappropriate Antidiuretic Hormone} Excessive release or effect of antidiuretic hormone causing impaired free-water excretion, low serum sodium, and inappropriately concentrated urine in the absence of an appropriate physiologic stimulus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sclerotherapy}A treatment in which an irritating or closing agent is injected into a blood vessel or body cavity to make it scar and seal, commonly used for varicose veins and selected vascular lesions.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Septum}A wall or partition separating two spaces, such as the nasal septum between the nasal cavities or the cardiac septum between heart chambers.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Serum}The clear, liquid part of blood that remains after clotting and contains water, electrolytes, proteins, hormones, antibodies, and other dissolved substances.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep Apnoea}Repeated pauses or reductions in breathing during sleep, most commonly because of upper-airway obstruction or less commonly inadequate respiratory drive.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Snoring}A noisy vibration of tissues in the upper airway during sleep, sometimes associated with obstructive sleep apnoea.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Smoking}Inhaling smoke from burning tobacco or other substances; it is a major cause of cardiovascular disease, chronic lung disease, cancer, and pregnancy complications.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Steatorrhoea}Excess fat in the stool, producing pale, bulky, greasy, foul-smelling stools that may be difficult to flush and indicate fat malabsorption.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Strangulated Hernia}A hernia in which the blood supply to the trapped tissue is cut off, causing ischaemia and possible tissue death; it is a surgical emergency.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subarachnoid Haemorrhage}Bleeding into the space between the arachnoid membrane and pia mater around the brain, classically causing a sudden severe thunderclap headache.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Subdural Haematoma}Accumulation of blood between the dura mater and arachnoid membrane, usually caused by tearing of bridging veins after head injury and sometimes developing gradually.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

It is not simply a high sex drive; assessment considers mood disorders, trauma, substance use, relationships, consent, and evidence-based behavioral treatment.

It includes consent, contraception, sexually transmitted infection prevention and testing, fertility, sexual function, safety, and respectful communication.

\medterm{Sharp Pains in Your Stomach During Pregnancy} Sharp abdominal pain in pregnancy may result from ligament stretching, gas, constipation, uterine contractions, urinary disease, ectopic pregnancy, placental complications, or other causes. Severe or persistent pain, bleeding, fever, faintness, vomiting, or reduced fetal movement needs urgent obstetric assessment.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shin Splints} Shin splints is common exertional pain along the inner border of the shin, often caused by increased training load, surface, footwear, or biomechanics. Rest from aggravating activity, gradual conditioning, and rehabilitation help; focal bone pain may indicate a stress injury.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shingles (Herpes Zoster)} Alternate index label for Herpes Zoster.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shingles and Pregnancy} Shingles is reactivation of varicella-zoster virus and usually causes a painful one-sided blistering rash. It is not the same as primary chickenpox exposure, but pregnancy requires prompt clinical advice about treatment, pain, and contact with people at risk.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shoulder Bursitis} Shoulder bursitis is inflammation of a fluid-filled sac around the shoulder, often associated with rotator-cuff disease or repetitive overhead activity. Pain with elevation and lying on the affected side is common; activity modification, therapy, medicines, injection, or treatment of the underlying cause may help.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Shulmans Syndrome (Eosinophilic Fasciitis)} Alternate index label for Eosinophilic Fasciitis.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sick Building Syndrome} Sick building syndrome describes nonspecific symptoms such as headache, fatigue, eye or throat irritation, cough, dizziness, or nausea associated with time in a building and improving away from it. Ventilation, humidity, pollutants, mold, and other environmental factors should be assessed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sickle Cell} Sickle-cell disease is an inherited hemoglobin disorder causing red cells to become rigid and sickle-shaped, leading to anemia, painful vaso-occlusive episodes, infection risk, stroke, acute chest syndrome, and organ damage. Comprehensive preventive and disease-modifying care is needed.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sickle Cell Anemia (Sickle Cell)} Alternate index label for Sickle Cell.

\medterm{Silent Sinus Syndrome} Silent sinus syndrome is painless inward collapse of the maxillary sinus and floor of the orbit, often causing facial asymmetry, enophthalmos, or a sunken eye. Imaging confirms the diagnosis, and endoscopic treatment restores sinus ventilation when indicated.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{silk road disease (Behcet's Syndrome)} Alternate index label for Behcet's Syndrome.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sinus Headache} Pain attributed to a sinus headache is often migraine, but sinus inflammation can cause facial pressure, congestion, reduced smell, and pain that worsens with bending. Fever, purulent discharge, persistent symptoms, or eye or neurologic signs guide evaluation.

\medterm{sixth disease (Roseola)} Alternate index label for Roseola.

\medterm{Skin Anatomy Picture Definition Function} Skin consists of epidermis, dermis, and subcutaneous tissue with appendages such as hair, nails, glands, nerves, vessels, and immune cells. It forms a barrier, regulates temperature, senses the environment, prevents fluid loss, and participates in immunity and vitamin D production.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Skin Cancer Overview} Skin cancer includes basal-cell carcinoma, squamous-cell carcinoma, melanoma, and less common tumors. Ultraviolet exposure, immune suppression, genetic susceptibility, and certain lesions increase risk; a changing, bleeding, nonhealing, or unusual lesion needs examination.

\medterm{SLE (Systemic Lupus)} Alternate index label for Systemic Lupus.

\textbf{Synonyms}:

\medterm{Sleep and Sleep Disorders in Children and Teens} Children and teens need age-appropriate sleep, and disorders may involve insomnia, circadian delay, sleep apnea, parasomnias, restless legs, or insufficient opportunity. Snoring, pauses in breathing, unusual daytime sleepiness, mood change, or poor school performance warrants evaluation.

\medterm{Sleep Disorder Periodic Limb Movement (Periodic Limb Movement Disorder)} Alternate index label for Periodic Limb Movement Disorder.
